## Global Data Watch

![](images/bcb67d6a9c6c0c4cc6876bf46aecc6723227fae65f7f31c05e1cc3f3ad35fffe.jpg)

Fed and ECB reinforce guidance for midyear ease; we see shallow easing

● BoJ return to orthodoxy points to rate hikes in 2H24

• Fading China<sup>’</sup>s strong start to the year

• Next week: Firm US PCE prices and Japan RS; Riksbank, SARB on hold

## Normal is as normal does

Buffeted by unprecedented shocks and dramatic policy actions, the current expansion has delivered extreme and unusual dynamics. Global inflation was pushed to multi-decade highs over 2022-23, during a recovery in which the level of global GDP has remained depressed relative to its pre-pandemic potential path. Central banks engaged in a synchronized roughly 500bp policy tightening (outside China) to contain inflation. While inflation has stepped down over the past year, core CPI remains elevated and global tightening has not, to date, produced a period of sub-potential global GDP growth or rising unemployment rates.

Guidance from central bankers today, however, suggests building confidence that unusual post-pandemic dynamics are giving way to a return to normalcy. To be sure, DM central bankers emphasize uncertainty and data dependence. And they recognize the possibility of lasting changes in performance—notably in the supply-side shifts incorporated in recent US (positive) and Western European (negative) central bank forecasts. But commentary and guidance have recently centered on a return to a “normal<sup>”</sup> in which gravitational forces return inflation to 2% (without damage to labor markets) and policy rates adjust toward neutral. Central bankers have, in turn, encouraged markets to embrace soft-landing optimism.

While the likelihood of a soft-landing outcome has increased, we are less optimistic that the Fed and ECB can deliver the substantial easing currently anticipated by markets and our bottom-up forecasts (Figure 1). In this regard, two assessments presented by Chair Powell this week are likely to be tested as the Fed and ECB express more conviction in a midyear start to easing.

Don<sup>’</sup>t get carried away by momentum. Underlying Chair Powell<sup>’</sup>s confidence that inflation is on a bumpy path to return to 2% is an extrapolation of recent trends where goods price deflation continues and is complemented by a drop in rent of shelter. By contrast, our focus on global impulses suggests goods price deflation is ending and that this shift will broadly offset the disinflationary impulse of moderating rent of shelter (Figure 2).

Forecasting wage and “supercore<sup>”</sup> services is more complicated, but we are less dismissive of the early-year firming than the Chair. We look for a 0.3%m/m rise in the February supercore PCE next week and see US wage and core service price inflation remaining at about a 4%ar during 1H24.

Catch a rising star. The Fed<sup>’</sup>s SEP nudged its long-term rate forecast higher, a development aligned with the rise in GDP growth and the median policy rate in 2025-26. However, Chair Powell downplayed easing financial conditions and asserted that the Fed<sup>’</sup>s policy continues to weigh on economic activity. The 525bp change in policy over 2022-23 dragged heavily on activity, but this component of monetary transmission appears to be fading fast— a point brought home by the recent stabilization in housing activity and moderating tightening in bank lending standards. The sustained drag from the current level of rates is less easy to tie down (Figure 3). Real rates are high relative to last cycle<sup>’</sup>s norms, but there are many reasons to view the post-GFC environment, in which powerful disinflationary drags—from deleveraging, fiscal tightening, and regulatory change—depressed neutral rates, as unusual.

Figure 3: US real policy rate and unemployment rate
![](images/dd3deebd29da522adb0cfb170365df36750cdfaa738abb54a2d5fe474af36f52.jpg)

Viewed from a broader historical perspective, the current level of real rates is not unusually high and stands well below the 3% level that has been historically associated with recession. Importantly, any consideration of the current neutral rate is colored by the Fed<sup>’</sup>s guidance that rates will be moving materially lower. Expectations that policy rates will follow (or perhaps exceed) any coming slide in inflation and not generate labor market easing is clearly diminishing the drag of the current policy stance.

## BoJ: a return to orthodoxy

While the Fed is biased to a return to previous norms, the BoJ signaled its confidence in Japan<sup>’</sup>s exit from a quarter century of deflation this week. It removed all major unconventional policy measures, exiting NIRP and abolishing YCC. Governor Ueda emphasized a return to an orthodox monetary framework with the policy rate as the primary tool. While the decision was wrapped in dovish near-term guidance, we do not expect a one-and-done move. Incoming data suggest firm underlying price momentum. And while the manufacturing sector is contracting this quarter, March surveys point to a midyear lift. We look for a jump in February retail sales next week to harken the beginning of a rebound in consumption. Pressure on the yen should reinforce the case for further hikes this year. While previous episodes of yen weakness have been met by currency intervention, Ueda<sup>’</sup>s comments this week suggested that currency moves that impacted the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s outlook assessment could lead to a policy response. In this case, the BoJ would likely hike earlier to avoid sparking further household capital outflows. In all, we expect the BoJ to hike 25bp in July and December.

## ECB and BoE tilt dovish

Dovish signals came from Western European central banks as well this week. As the Swiss National Bank kicked off the DM easing cycle, the ECB strengthened its signal for a June cut. The BoE meeting saw two hawkish dissenters drop their votes for higher rates. Although the ECB is inclined to begin easing at midyear, President Lagarde warned that subsequent cuts are data-dependent. In this regard, the data are biased in the direction of a limited easing. The final February HICP report confirmed that inflation firmed at the start of the year with surprising strength in services. Separately, the flash March PMI moved upwards, consistent with our forecast for a 1H24 growth rebound. While not challenging the ECB<sup>’</sup>s GDP forecast, surveys point to steady hiring—a challenge to their forecast for a productivity revival. For the BoE, risks have shifted earlier from communication but we continue to expect the first ease in August. Slow progress is expected on services inflation and this week<sup>’</sup>s flash PMI reinforced upside risks to growth.

## DM diversity in easing timing

Outside of the G4, there is more diversity in DM central bank guidance. Australia<sup>’</sup>s February labor market data were strong and the RBA does not see policy as overly restrictive—developments that point to a later start to the easing process than current market pricing. Norges Bank is hawkish among Western European central banks and guided toward a September rate cut this week. Despite a weaker core inflation forecast, FX concerns dominate the policy debate. This stands in contrast with the Riksbank, which may signal an elevated chance of a May cut next week given Sweden<sup>’</sup>s recessionary dynamics. In Canada, core inflation measures surprised lower for February. That would put more risk toward an April cut given the weak growth backdrop if not for earlier hawkish BoC rhetoric; we see a firm case for cutting in June.

## China: Lifting but not celebrating

China Jan-Feb activity data were strong with broad-based surprises delivered in IP, infrastructure investment, and retail spending (Figure 4). While prompting an upward revision to current quarter growth to 6.6% (q/q, saar), we maintain a cautious attitude for the coming quarters and still anticipate growth cooling toward a 4%ar. Although solid global demand should sustain China<sup>’</sup>s export and factory output growth, there is less confidence that domestic demand will deliver solid gains. Weakness in the property sector persists alongside sustained deflation. As a result of declining GDP prices, corporate profits remain under pressure. With household income expectations still depressed and the survey-based unemployment rate moving higher, the consumption pickup at the start of the year looks likely to prove transitory. Policy support could make a difference for domestic demand—but beyond a recent proposal to encourage electric vehicle trade-ins, there is no evidence of a shift taking place. The bigger risk is that fiscal and monetary supports fade by midyear and we repeat last year<sup>’</sup>s profile, in which 1Q delivers the economy<sup>’</sup>s strongest performance.

Figure 4: China activity indicators
![](images/3615ad78f02cd28eee63bae360288e9d64c4739551e5cf54e1be02520f8e832d.jpg)

## Variation also evident across EM CBs

As a group, EM central banks hiked earlier and more aggressively than those in the DM, allowing for earlier and more extensive easing cycles for a number of economies in LatAm and EMEA. Some of those that are fairly advanced in their easing cycles are starting to slow their pace as they approach their terminal rates. For others who have yet to start cutting rates, the prospect of a shallow Fed easing cycle may influence the timing or extent of easing by their respective central banks.

This week Banxico kicked off its easing cycle with a 25bp cut, but unexpectedly guided toward a steady pace of further easing at upcoming meetings. We now expect the current 11% policy rate to reach 9.5% at end-2024 and 7.5% at end-2025. Much weaker near-term growth (we revised 1Q to -0.5%ar from 3%) is supporting a dovish stance. Colombia<sup>’</sup>s BanRep accelerated its easing pace with a 50bp move this week.

Following this week<sup>’</sup>s upside surprise to inflation in South Africa, we shifted the start to the SARB<sup>’</sup>s shallow easing cycle from May to September, postponing a third rate cut to next year. In Israel, inflation momentum is rising as the economy rebounds from the war shock. We still expect the BoI to cut a further 75bp this year, but firming inflation skews risks toward fewer cuts.

We continue to expect rate cuts by EM Asian central banks to follow those by the Fed. Recent performance reinforces this view as export growth has been easing, evidenced most recently in Korea<sup>’</sup>s flash trade data for March. Moreover, the transmission to household income from the capital-intensive tech sector has been limited. Easing household incomes have helped pull down core services inflation across much of the region. Risks to this view come from stronger China growth and an upward bias for commodity prices—particularly oil. Indicative of these risks, a jump in Taiwan's inflation led to a surprise 12.5bp rate hike by the CBC this week.

Meanwhile, Brazil and Peru have turned more cautious as their easing cycles advance. As we anticipated, BCB cut 50bp but only committed to another 50bp in May; policy should be data dependent thereafter. Peru<sup>’</sup>s BCRP paused, but we expect additional easing in May.

## Türkiye: CBRT surprises with 500bp hike

The CBRT front-loaded a 500bp rate hike, taking the policy rate to 50% this week, in response to a deterioration in the inflation outlook and a decline in FX reserves. The CBRT also set the overnight lending rate at 53%, doubling the width of the interest rate corridor to ±300bp. The timing of the decision just ahead of the March municipal elections, alongside hawkish guidance, sends a strong signal that the CBRT is prepared to hike again if needed. We also believe the CBRT is in no rush to ease policy and we look for rate cuts to begin in 4Q24. We maintain our end-2024 policy rate forecast of 45% and this commitment to fight inflation puts downward risks to our end-2024 inflation forecast of 44.7%.

For additional detail, see our full policy rate QED report.

## Economic Activity Tracking

See here for a real-time snapshot and methodology reports.

Our forecast for 1Q24 global real GDP growth ticked up 0.2%-pt this week to 2.8%ar (Figure 1). The top-down global nowcaster moved sideways at 1.9%ar, suggesting 0.9%-pt of downside risk to global growth (Table 2).

Nearly two-thirds of the economies we track have seen data surprise stronger over the past 30 days. The nowcaster implies upside risk to 1Q growth in just over half of these economies (Figure 2).

• Our CapexNow model estimate for 1Q24 global capex growth increased 0.3%-pt to 1.7%ar this week (Table 4).

This week we updated our retail sales and IP aggregates through January. Global consumer goods spending maintained its decelerated—but still positive—growth path, while global factory output continued to weaken. Encouragingly, the DM flash PMIs sent another signal of manufacturing improvement in March.

## J.P. Morgan Market Watch

## Bonds

Bonds partially retraced some of the previous week<sup>’</sup>s sell-off following a more dovish Fed and a somewhat dovish BoJ outcome as it kept Rinban purchases unchanged even as it ended negative rates and YCC. We continue to see the Fed starting its cutting cycle in June, though the updated projections imply a shallower path. We note that even in shallower easing cycles yields tend to decline significantly leading up to the first cut and continue to recommend 5s/30s steepeners that benefit from a transition to easing, but remain patient before adding outright longs.

In the Euro area, bonds rallied in part on a dovish Fed, and while domestic data showed some pickup ECB commentary suggests a growing consensus for a June cut. We stay long 5Y Germany and hold steepeners in 10Yx15Y Germany and 2Yx2Y/10Yx10Y on the EUR swap curve. In the UK, despite the drop of hawkish dissent we don<sup>’</sup>t view the March BoE meeting as a signal to turn bullish on duration. We are tactically short May24 MPC OIS and hold Aug24/Nov24 MPC OIS curve flatteners. In Japan, despite the removal of negative rates and YCC, we are cautious in adding steepeners given uncertainty over near-term Rinban amounts and ahead of potential quarter-end rebalancing flows.

In EM, a dovish Fed removes a significant event risk for EM assets. As activity indicators support ongoing upward revisions to global growth, this sets a more supportive backdrop for EM to price out risk premia for a more hawkish Fed. We stay OW EM local duration with OWs in Brazil, Colombia, Uruguay, Czechia, Poland and Indonesia, partially offset by a short duration overlay and UW in Romania (EM Fixed Income Focus, Mar 21<sup>st</sup>).

## Credit

For now, stable yields means tighter US HG spreads. A dovish sounding Fed, US HG yields back in the 5.50% sweet spot, slowing issuance versus still strong demand saw HG spreads rally back to the YTD tights. Supply has already begun to slow somewhat and this should improve further. Demand remains strong with 20 consecutive weeks of IG fund inflows. The FOMC meeting earlier was marginally positive for credit as well with overall dovish tone leading to some steepening of the UST curve which should further boost demand for long-end credit as investors look to lock-in yields for the cycle. As well, the historic move from BoJ of the removal of YCC and its major unconventional monetary policy measures all at once also seemed to be priced in and and the market appears to be emphasizing the dovish elements of the meeting (CMOS, Mar 22<sup>nd</sup>). The negative aspect from

tighter BoJ policy will be if long-end JGB yields rise much more significantly than they have to date, which would create more domestic alternatives to buying foreign fixed income assets. Given the BoJ language around their intent to purchase JGBs as needed to prevent a rapid rise in long-end yields, it seems unlikely that JGB yields will rise faster than hedging costs decline but this is certainly a risk. However, we believe it is far more of a risk for UST demand than for USD HG credit demand from Japanese investors (BoJ policy shifts and US HG Credit, Mar 20<sup>th</sup>).

Figure 1: Year-to-date returns %
![](images/342675f54575407c5c6337a2ab406b7ba6143d80487cb83d185da69ad3f8135f.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan Global Economics

We believe that euro credit has hit a floor at current valuations, which means that the risk-reward for being OW is unattractive. Central bank meetings propelled equity markets to new highs, with euro IG and HY actually 1bp and 16bp wider on the week, respectively. We see this as evidence that we are at the floor for valuations, which means that the riskreward is unattractive with any deviation from the <sup>‘</sup>soft landing<sup>’</sup> narrative in either direction posing a threat to spreads. We continue to recommend setting iTraxx Main hedges (European Credit Weekly, Mar 22<sup>nd</sup>).

Euro HY market constituents are either trading at extreme tights, or wides, with very little in between. The distressed cohort has become more concentrated due to the presence of a small number of larger capital structures. These lumpier exposures pose upside risk to our 2.5% FY24 default rate forecast. Unlike previous occasions, prominent idiosyncratic events have not spilled over into the broader euro HY market. We suspect this continues for now due to strong supply-demand technicals, but remain uncomfortable with valuations medium term. The bigger problem we see is that returns are likely to underwhelm without a strong showing from the CCC cohort (High Yield Talking Points, Mar 22<sup>nd</sup>).

## Currencies

An uncomfortable setup for USD bulls and bears. Two near-term evolving issues are relevant: the pro-cyclical lift from improving China and European growth, which would normally be USD bearish, but will be hard to monetize in this cycle. Ongoing yen weakness despite the BoJ rate hike; if this extends further, it would spill into FX more broadly with Asian and Antipodean FX most vulnerable. Our medium-term bias still remains bullish USD. We discuss Japan<sup>’</sup>s policy alternatives to counter yen weakness.

Macro Trade Recommendations: Sell CAD vs basket of USD & AUD to isolate CAD weakness after CPI. Take profit on tactical short-NOK basket, but still bearish. Stopped out of EUR/JPY downside and unwind deep OTM USD/JPY digi put; hold CHF/JPY downside. Stay short CHF vs SEK after SNB. Net long USD though in modest size; neutral GBP. Long SEK but EUR/SEK downside expired OTM. Stay OW EM FX (FXMW, Mar 22<sup>nd</sup>).

## Commodities

Global upstream oil and gas capex on target to reach \$555bn in 2024. Global oil upstream E&P producers are expected to develop a record 37 bn barrels of oil in 2024, 2 bn more than the prior peak in 2014. The latest investment guidance from oil and gas producers is up 85% from the 2020 lows, although still 21% below the 2014 peak. Total oil supply is projected to rise by about 7% in 2024 compared to 2014. A key reason behind the steady growth in production despite significant reduction in equipment levels is the continued advancement of producer and service provider efficiencies in drilling and completions. In short, operators can produce more with less capital (Oil Markets Weekly, Mar 21<sup>st</sup>).

Russia<sup>’</sup>s offline refining capacity rises to 900 kbd. Ukraine intensified its assault on Russian oil-processing facilities over the weekend. Two refineries were reportedly heavily damaged, suggesting both plants could be halted completely. Since the start of the year, there were 23 drone attacks on Russian energy facilities—two on oil terminals, four on oil and fuel depots, and 14 on refiners. While damages at most facilities were repaired relatively quickly, almost 900 kbd of refining capacity remains offline likely for several weeks if not months (Oil Flash Note, Mar 18<sup>th</sup>).

Global commodity market open interest hits six-month high as crude oil and copper inflows surge. The estimated value of open interest across global commodity markets surged by 6% to a six-month high of \$1.33 trillion, driven by a significant increase in price and contract flows across crude oil and base metals along with a rise in agri prices. Gold prices have consolidated above \$2,150/oz and are trading more than \$140/oz above levels implied by recent changes in the USD and US real yields (Commodity Market Positioning & Flows, Mar 19<sup>th</sup>).

## US: The economic effects of surging immigration

Under a variety of estimates immigration has surged in the last 2-3 years

The most immediate implication is an increase in the potential workforce and pace of job growth

C It has also supported unusually strong homebuilding and lifted state and local government spending

The inflation implications of the immigration surge are ambiguous

Immigration has soared over the last two or three years, profoundly affecting a number of the most widely-followed economic statistics. It<sup>’</sup>s been important to the surprising pace of job growth, even alongside a modestly increasing unemployment rate. This, in turn, has been one factor behind surprisingly strong overall income and output growth. Implications for per-capita income are much more ambiguous, and wage gains for lower-skilled workers could suffer. Elevated immigration has been a factor supporting robust homebuilding activity. Spending for immigrant social services has also been behind the surprisingly strong outlays by state and local governments, though a quantification is elusive. At a high level the increase in immigration probably hasn<sup>’</sup>t had a first order effect on inflation: immigrants add to labor supply, but also add to consumer demand.

## Strong to quite strong

Interest in the economic aspects of the current immigration surge was piqued by a recent CBO report which estimated that immigration was adding 3.3 million people to the US population per year in 2023 and 2024, over three times the average number in the prior decade (Figure 1).

Figure 1: CBO immigration estimates, by category
![](images/bf96a016babf2a0d03ce82b02dfb4ed3c606cee2e03ef9e28abc842db68d0c38.jpg)

CBO divides immigrants into three categories: lawful permanent residents—mostly green card holders—INA status— mostly students and others on temporary visas—and the “other<sup>”</sup> category. This latter category includes those who entered the country illegally or who overstayed their visas.

A principal data source for the “other<sup>”</sup> immigrants is the Customs and Border Patrol data on people encountered by the CBP, nearly all of whom were released into the country (Figure 2). This data has moved higher the last few years, exceeding 300,000 per month recently.

Figure 2: US Border Patrol monthly nationwide encounters 000s
![](images/69a9c54218ebe70191fc1e6c6b3c4af8ddba8d73ed5677fda62e719c05bf997c.jpg)

To be sure, even before the CBO report it was widely understood that immigration was picking up, and the household survey of the employment report (formally called the Current Population Survey) was indicating that this was becoming an important source of labor force growth (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Civilian noninstitutional population -- Current Pop. Survey 000s, over year-ago ch. in 12mma
![](images/2643f4a3b856d931200fd913a1129b0e64dfce04fc0c07b408dad2809e6d1b37.jpg)

The population estimates in the household survey are provided by the Census using the American Community Survey (ACS), currently available through 2022. In addition to timeliness, there are other concerns about the quality of the immigration numbers from the ACS.

However, the employment report can be used to infer another estimate of population growth. In the household survey mismeasured population estimates will mostly divide out in ratios like the employment-to-population ratio. Meanwhile the establishment survey measure of employment is regularly benchmarked to the administrative data from state unemployment insurance programs. Because the penalty for not paying UI tax is generally greater than for hiring an undocumented worker, this is often considered to be a thorough tabulation of paid employment. (Asylum seekers and humanitarian parolees can work legally after a few months).

From these two facts, one can then divide the establishment measure of employment by the households employment-topopulation ratio to arrive at an alternate (civilian noninstitutional) population estimate. Over the past two years this implies population growth in this segment near four million per year, above the Census estimate but in the same ballpark of what is implied by the CBO figures (Figure 4).

Figure 4: Civilian noninstitutionalized population growth
![](images/2e8801b3c54dfd5bf46179f2cd8468f9f0927fd54c88a3c4270816090f17000f.jpg)

## Now it makes more sense

The jump in immigration helps resolve several of the puzzles about the macroeconomy over the past few years.

Strong employment growth but looser labor markets. If civilian noninstitutional employment growth is running closer to four million—as CBO and the establishment survey imply—rather than two million—as Census estimates—then the demographically determined sustainable level of employment growth just to meet new workforce entrants is closer to 200,000 per month rather than 100,000 per month.

Faster trend growth. Even if output per worker is lower among foreign-born workers than native ones—which is almost certainly the case for the CBO<sup>’</sup>s “other<sup>”</sup> category— then the added workers should boost output growth for as long as the immigration surge continues. (Relatedly, the recent pick-up in productivity looks even more impressive as labor quality may be adversely impacted by the mix of new workers).

Stronger state and local spending. State and local government activity has been an outperforming sector recently. There are likely many causes behind this, but immigration assistance is surely one of those causes. While there<sup>’</sup>s no thorough tabulation across the thousands of municipalities, one example close to home provides a hint: New York City has budgeted \$12 billion between now and FY25 to provide for

migrant needs. Even if other cities and states are budgeting proportionally much less, the sums are likely to be non-trivial from a macroeconomic perspective.

Strong homebuilding but tight housing markets. A few years ago it was thought that household formation trends implied that the demand for new housing units by now might be just over one million per year. Even if the average household size for immigrants is greater than that for natives, it<sup>’</sup>s hard to see how the added demand for housing units is much less than 500,000 per year. This may help explain why even with housing completions recently running just below 1.5 million saar, homeowner and rental vacancy rates have barely budged off their multidecade lows.

Ambiguous inflation implications. More immigrants mean more labor supply and more consumer demand. At a first pass it<sup>’</sup>s not obvious this outward shift in the aggregate supply and demand curves should have any effect on prices or inflation. One can add nuance. In a disinflationary direction, foreignborn participation rates run above 4-5%-points above native participation rates. (Though a recent Brookings study notes participation rates are lower for more recently-arrived immigrants). Additionally, remittances abroad should lower their increment to domestic aggregate demand.

Going in the inflationary direction, immigrants (and lower-income individuals more generally) are likely to have higher marginal propensities to consume out of income. The state and local spending issue discussed above would further increase the boost to aggregate demand. Finally, planning lags, permitting bottlenecks, and other transitional issues mean that housing inflation could be aggravated by immigrant household formation in the near-term, even if in principle such immigration is neutral for rents and home prices in the longer-term.

## Looking ahead

Immigration is going to be an important issue in the upcoming election, perhaps the most important issue. If Trump wins, his administration would likely return to the immigration policies he enacted during his previous term with an emphasis on border security, but subject to continued, and in particular post-pandemic, legal challenges. However, even if Biden wins the recent bipartisan border agreement may become more likely to pass Congress, which would also change immigration policy, albeit more gradually. Biden, if reelected, may also have more flexibility to take executive action on the issue, as well. In either case we are hesitant to extrapolate the important economic effects of the recent surge in immigration beyond the end of this calendar year.

## Europe: Bank CRE problems unlikely to impact growth

• European bank exposures to CRE globally are much lower than those of US banks

• Euro CRE loss rates relatively lower on higher office space occupancy and less “originate-to-distribute”

• Stress tests suggest European banks can absorb potential CRE losses without endangering capital adequacy

High net interest income, improving risk perceptions to offset any CRE impact on bank lending standards

• CRE losses could amplify impact of a broader downturn on banks...

• ...but solid household and corporate balance sheets provide a significant shield

Nearly a year after the collapse of Silicon Valley Bank, bank CRE exposures are back in focus following troubles at New York Community Bank (NYCB). Specialied real estate lenders in Germany (Aareal Bank and Deutsche Pfandbriefbank) have come under the market microscope in Europe, although CRE-related risks have been a focus for regulators and supervisors for a while. Indeed, ECB Vice President Luis de Guindos at the monetary policy press conference of March 7 reiterated that it is one of the main risks to financial stability. The big question is whether credit risk associated with CRE exposures could be a systemic issue for European banks and lead to a material retrenchment in credit provision. Given the bank-based nature of financial intermediation in Europe, this could have a severe impact on the growth outlook.

European bank exposures to CRE globally are much lower than those of US banks (around 10 percent of loans versus 25 percent), although there is of course significant heterogeneity across banks and countries. Scope for trouble in the CRE space seems higher in the US than in Europe, as office space occupancy has recovered more in European cities versus US ones. Also, the “originate-to-distribute<sup>”</sup> of CRE exposures in the US into CMBS potentially foreshadows higher loss rates than the “originate to hold<sup>”</sup> model in Europe.

European banks have high capital and liquidity ratios, creating significant buffers for adverse scenarios. And stress tests carried out by our Equity and Credit colleagues suggest that CRE losses could eat into profits but not lead to a significant capital hit. A key trade-off is that while high interest rates create CRE stress, especially given the shorter maturity of CRE loans, they also lead to a higher net interest margin. With interest rate cuts expected to start this year, the upward pressure on refinancing costs should also decline, helping reduce credit risk. More generally, bank lending standards are correlated with broader risk perceptions linked to economic activity rather than just the cost of funding or balance sheet changes. To the extent that broader risk perceptions are improving (in line with the PMI), this should offset any impact of CRE related stress.

Overall, we don<sup>’</sup>t expect CRE stress to metastasise into a significant growth impact, although there is likely to be localised bank stress, including because of the opacity of CRE valuations and loans to non-bank financial institutions (NBFIs) that are exposed. It could, however, act as an amplifier if there is a broader economic downturn and problems in more systemically important sectors (e.g., residential real estate). Although given the state of household and corporate balance sheets, we don<sup>’</sup>t view the latter as a high probability event.

## Bigger CRE role in US than in Europe

In general, CRE plays a larger role in the economy in the US and in terms of bank lending. According to IMF estimates from the October 2023 GFSR, total CRE debt is around 18 percent of GDP in the US and 12 percent of GDP in Europe. And as Table 1 shows, CRE loans are close to 25 percent of total loans for all US banks (and c.40 percent for small US banks). Figure 1 shows the share of CRE loans for various euro area countries. There is of course heterogeneity, although in general the share is below 10 percent (except for Greece and Austria that have shares slightly above 10 percent). For Nordic banks, it is also around 10 percent.

Table 1: US exposure to commercial real estate \$tn, %, 23Q3
<table><tr><td></td><td>US (AII)</td><td>US (Small)</td></tr><tr><td>Commercial real estate loans</td><td>2.9</td><td>1.9</td></tr><tr><td>Total loans</td><td>12.2</td><td>4.4</td></tr><tr><td>Total assets</td><td>22.9</td><td>6.5</td></tr><tr><td>CRE % Loans</td><td>24.1</td><td>44.3</td></tr><tr><td>CRE % Assets</td><td>12.9</td><td>29.7</td></tr></table>

Source: Federal Reserve, J.P. Morgan

Figure 1: CRE loans by country % of total loans, 23Q3
![](images/c00dfd0ae4940bc09b39972b2ffe3294867e6e32c84c73d3282d77957f593ef9.jpg)
Source: EBA, J.P. Morgan

Marked shift in CRE valuations and activity

Since 2022 there has been a sizeable shift in the CRE sector. As reported in the ECB November 2023 Financial Sector Stability report, there was a near 50 percent drop in the number of CRE transactions in 2023H1. This has impeded price discovery and there is generally less transparency about pricing and valuations in the CRE sector, making comparisons hard. Nevertheless, it is clear that commercial real estate prices have declined more than residential real estate prices (Figure 2). Declines have been particularly notable in the Nordics and Germany.

Figure 2: Euro area property prices
![](images/0003507ee9d140f8a041f8938c4d47428795d96b77ff66a8d49eceab5f40819b.jpg)

REIT valuations suggest major corrections across all sectors, with prices at least 25 percent below pre pandemic levels except for the industrial sector (Figure 3). Across all countries, REIT valuations have dropped (Figure 4), and the declines are over 50 percent from pre pandemic levels in Italy and Spain, although differences in sectoral make-up of national indexes makes cross country comparisons difficult.

Figure 3: REIT indices by property type Idx, 28/02/2020 = 100
![](images/ee2acb9489d1a6ce0ed68b810d799a294f1ad3fe2e541ac17e787cb00051bbca.jpg)

Figure 4: REIT indices by country % chg from February 2020
![](images/070040f1e99eb422832177ea82e88e2c8f575ae28ab249b6e1e8f9f963d4d413.jpg)

Accounting practices are also probably allowing for the slow recognition of losses. For example, some reports suggest that the low level of NPLs in Germany could be in part because real estate valuers there use a long-term approach that smooths pricing on the assumption that most investors don<sup>’</sup>t sell in a falling market. German banks also update valuations of buildings they have financed less regularly than peers in the US or UK, so problems can be masked for longer. Typically, when loans are refinanced, that should be the trigger for revaluation of property values.

## European banks in good financial shape

Consistent with the view we articulated in June last year, European banks are in very good shape. The ECB hiking cycle has led to a tremendous increase in net interest income as loan rates have gone up much more than deposit rates (Figure 5). Bank stock prices have gone up considerably (Figure 6) and price to book ratios have moved up in tandem, although they still remain well below 1 (Figure 7).

Figure 5: Euro area banks
![](images/32724a41bf0eb8db8d0e158f77f502bda14e886318f337f7a914065512ded504.jpg)

Figure 6: Euro area vs US bank stock prices Index, 6th Mar 2023 = 100
![](images/9d957ec49f509b0c271a64d94cf674c9af0764e8f96c95e88b0002cccbb535a0.jpg)

Figure 7: Price-to-book ratios of Euro area and US banks ratio
![](images/c4724d53defff5b8f8517334481ad4215c1c9356069cd5c8446a27eb86914191.jpg)

Capital and liquidity coverage ratios remain high (well above minimum regulatory thresholds) and NPLs are low (Figures 8-10). According to the 2023 EBA Transparency exercise, loan-to-value ratios (LTVs) remain at reasonable levels for CRE exposures in European banks.

Figure 8: Common equity tier 1 ratios
![](images/d9a263cdaccaa2062711075fb50d9632d0a815c172b0305b68056937d1b9ed8c.jpg)

![](images/360420477f46952b09700847889813e77011d2354aabfb7205d7bdf732908eda.jpg)

Figure 10: Non-performing loan ratios
![](images/1aca77c34ce6a607e579dbfd156d8ea24ff00f905fb54d95a388608594b7819b.jpg)

Around two-thirds of the exposures have an LTV of less than 60 percent, providing a cushion in case of a particularly marked correction in CRE valuations. Still, around EUR 160bn of CRE loans have an LTV of greater than 100 percent. Countries with the highest share of loans with LTVs above 100 percent are mainly in emerging Europe (shares around or greater than 30 percent). In the Euro area, Italy has a share above 15 percent, while it is below 10 percent in France and Germany.

## CRE stress tests suggest banks resilient

The EBA 2023 stress test (covering 70 banks from 16 EU and EEA countries, of which 57 are from the Euro area) had an adverse scenario including over three years: (i) a 6 p.p. cumulative EU GDP decline and unemployment rate increase; (ii) a near 200 bps increase in long-term interest rates; and (iii) close to a 30 percent decline in CRE prices. But the banking system was resilient under this adverse scenario. The key point emerging was that higher interest rates increase net interest margins and income as well as credit risk. And, the average maturity of CRE loans is much lower than for other loans (over 40 percent have a maturity of less than a year), so they reprice more quickly.

Recent JP Morgan stress tests conducted by our equity and credit teams also suggest risks to capital depletion are limited, although earnings could take a major hit. Applying the US office loss rate to all office exposures (8 percent) and a 2 percent loss rate on other CRE exposures (consistent with EBA 2023 stress test loss rates) suggests no single bank in the sample included (major benchmark banks across the large European countries) would suffer any erosion of capital. Additional office holdings<sup>’</sup> stress tests for the small German banks suggests that acute stress would be largely contained to the specialist lender segment and should not materially impact the Landesbanks.

Moreover, an 8 percent loss rate may be too high for European office space exposures given it is based off US CMBS experience, and the “originate to hold<sup>”</sup> model is much more prevalent in Europe. In particular, bank lenders are: (a) likely to have more conservative underwriting standards than CMBS sponsors; (b) not be constrained by time limited vehicles like CMBS structures, allowing them the option to restructure loans more easily; and (c) typically face lower complexity when engaging with a borrower (may be conflicts of interest between classes of CMBS investors). Finally, office vacancies not as high in European cities as US ones, with more workers returning to the office in Europe and Asia relative to the US.

One area where stress could potentially show up is in wider spreads on dollar cross currency swaps. This is because dollar liquidity coverage ratios (LCRs) have been persistently below 100 percent for many years for European banks (the reverse is true for EUR and GBP LCRs). Hence, US office losses of the bigger European banks (apart from the specialist German lenders, its mostly large European banks like Deutsche Bank and Santander that have significant US office portfolios, and they tend to be in the downtown areas of large US cities) could lead to a scramble for dollar funding and further recourse to cross currency swaps.

## Growth impact likely limited

Europe has more bank-based financial intermediation than the US (Figure 11). Thus, a bank lending crunch should have a more material growth impact. There is also a strong correlation between bank lending standards and the credit impulse (Figure 12). Hence, a key question is what would be the likely impact of CRE troubles on bank lending standards?

Figure 11: NFCs loans as % of total corporate debt %, both scales
![](images/a7a8377c1249b6988663d253f496aa2fae2d07a52bf81756b8085543a41b1427.jpg)

Figure 12: Euro area credit impulse and lending standards Net % of bank tightening their lending standards Ch. %oya
![](images/512559defff2245fd3da8bad3a3b21334d79e7e1e4d8ddbc2be73feaa59bd4f2.jpg)

Overall, we do not assign a high probability to CRE-related stress leading to a material tightening of bank lending standards, and so it is not in our baseline scenario. As mentioned above, banks are in very good financial shape, and stress tests suggest they have buffers to withstand CRE-related losses. Moreover, even if credit losses go up, to the extent that they are caused by “high interest rates for long<sup>”</sup> there should be a significant offset from high net interest income. And, if Western European policy rates decline in line with market and analyst expectations (around 100bps over the next year give or take), real estate firms and landlords should be able to refinance existing CRE loans at lower interest rates, reducing credit risk.

Importantly, lending standards are also correlated with broader risk perceptions rather than just the cost of funding or balance sheet reasons, and indeed are closely correlated with the PMI (Figure 13). The PMI has picked up in early 2024 in Western Europe, suggesting that lending standards may continue to tighten less or indeed loosen soon. Our baseline forecast is for Western European growth to gradually pick up over 2024, which combined with lower interest rates would reduce risk perceptions further.

Figure 13: ECB lending standards and composite PMI DI, sa % net
![](images/5711dacc397ddbab7709af7588356e7896adb9e11fcc44ca773a65de769b40bb.jpg)

Of course, there are other scenarios that could play out. Valuations suffer from significant opacity in the CRE sector, and the sector is not just confronting high interest rates and increased construction costs, but structural issues such as changes in work practices and the climate transition. This could potentially mean an even bigger decline in CRE prices than assumed in the EBA stress tests (nearly 30 percent over three years). Bank could also face stress via NBFI exposure to CRE if they have large borrowings from banks (e.g., investment funds and insurers).

EBA data suggest that EU/EEA banks<sup>’</sup> direct exposure towards NBFIs is around 7 percent of their total assets and highly concentrated in a few large banks, although these banks also typically have more diversified assets. CRE is also a highly cyclical sector, so if the broader economy has a major downturn, then CRE losses could certainly amplify banking stresses originating elsewhere. Overall, however, we believe the strength of household and corporate balance sheets, and the tightness of labour markets, provides significant protection for now against broader banking stress.

## ECB: A demand-driven narrow-corridor system

• ECB has concluded a 15-month review of its operational framework

• As expected, it has opted for a demand-driven system, where banks decide on how many reserves to take

• A narrow corridor will limit any upward drift of €STR

• In future, longer-term refis and a structural asset porfolio will add liquidity; size and form not yet set

Last week, the ECB concluded a 15-month review of its operational framework and announced some changes to prepare for the time when excess reserves may no longer be high enough to keep overnight monetary market rates pegged around the deposit facility rate. In brief, the ECB has opted for a demand-driven system, where the marginal reserve injected into the system depends on the demand of the banks. As this reduces the ECB<sup>’</sup>s control over the level of excess reserves, overnight interest rates may see an upward drift above the deposit rate, which the ECB will limit by simply narrowing the the lower part of the corridor from 50bp to 15bp. And to better balance the overall provision of liquidity, the regular refinancing operations will eventually be supplemented with longer-term refinancing operations and a structural asset portfolio. Neither the size nor the form of these has been decided, but they will play only supporting roles and could pursue “green<sup>”</sup> objectives. Overall, the changes are modest and preserve many features of the existing system that have served the ECB well.

## A demand-driven narrow-corridor system

The basic issue the ECB had to consider was how to control overnight money market rates when the level of excess reserves falls to much lower levels. At present, they are still very high at €3,500bn, but three factors will reduce them over time. First, banks will repay another €400bn of TLTROs in the course of this year. Second, the ECB<sup>’</sup>s APP and PEPP holdings will reduce likely by around €500bn per year as QT proceeds. Third, banknotes in circulation are continuing to rise and banks must “borrow<sup>”</sup> these from the ECB by using reserves. According to Isabel Schnable, excess reserves may decline to €2,100bn by the end of next year and likely fall further thereafter.

As the level of excess reserves falls, the downward pressure they exert on overnight money market rates will begin to diminish. This is because any demand for reserves from a bank or nonbank that happens to be short of reserves at the end of the business day will be met by a smaller volume of offers from banks that still have excess reserves. The experience from 2010-11 and 2013-14 suggests that overnight money market rates begin to drift above the deposit rate when excess reserves falls below €500bn (Figure 1).

Figure 1: ECB policy interest rates and level of excess reserves
![](images/219c57b218353c0919cfeed1d3613a96baef98d6d3b39f1f6d80f1d8c4ec8f4e.jpg)

The ECB<sup>’</sup>s operational framework review asked how such a situation should be handled. One option was to stop excess reserves from falling in the first place by maintaining a large asset portfolio. But, this Fed-style solution has the disadvantage of effectively requiring the ECB to maintain large holdings of government bonds, with all the usual complexities of doing so in a 20-country monetary union. In addition, reserves forced into the system through asset purchases may eventually flow to some banks and leaving other banks short of reserves.

Another option was to stick closely to the current system, whilst narrowing the interest rate corridor to put a tighter bound around any upward drift of overnight interest rates. This is what the ECB has decided on. The main difference to the Fed-style solution is in how the marginal reserve is injected into the system. In the Fed<sup>’</sup>s case, that decision will be made by the Fed itself as it controls how many bonds it buys. In the ECB<sup>’</sup>s case, that decision will be left to the banks. The ECB will simply offer banks very regular opportunities to obtain as many reserves as they want through one-week and three-month refinancing operations. But, it will not control the actual take-up, thereby accepting the risk that the level of excess reserve may not be high enough to keep overnight rates at around the deposit rate.

A key benefit is that this preserves all parts of the current system that have served the ECB well in past episodes of banking system stress. First, every single Euro area bank will still have access to as much liquidity as it needs at the main refi rate, i.e. the ECB will continue with full allotment at these operations. Second, banks will be able to obtain that liquidity against a very wide pool of collateral, including many nonmarketable assets such as individual loans. Hence, the ECB<sup>’</sup>s balance sheet will continue to act as a shock absorber, reducing the liquidity risks for banks.

As noted, the downside of this demand-driven system is that the ECB does not directly control the level of excess reserves and hence where overnight interest rates trade relative to the deposit rate. But, the easy and pragmatic solution is to narrow the corridor. At present, the main refinancing rate is currently 50bp above the deposit rate and this spread will be reduced on 18 September to just 15bp. In practical terms, it is the main refi rate that will be cut, rather than the deposit rate raised. The choice of 15bp is to strike a balance between (a) giving some incentive for interbank lending and (b) keeping overnight rates within in a narrow band so that the ECB has sufficient control over the transmission of its policy stance. The ECB thinks that a 15bp corridor width will incentivize sufficient interbank lending and lead to a sufficient reserve demand to keep overnight rates close to the deposit rate.

In some sense, the two solutions are not that different as a Fed-style supply-driven floor system could be supplemented with standing facilities that still give individual banks access to reserves should they face a shortage even though the banking system as a whole has a lot of excess reserves. But, the ECB clearly did not want a large-scale asset portfolio to be the key part of the system. Instead, the ECB will not aim for a certain level of excess reserves and will tolerate some upward drift of overnight money market rates if banks do not demand enough reserves, but its narrow corridor sufficiently limits any such drift so that the policy stance can still be controlled.

## Two optional extras for another day

While the focus is on making reserves available to banks at relatively short maturities, the ECB will consider supplementing this with some longer-term refinancing operations and with a structural asset portfolio. No decision has been taken about these in terms of size and composition. However, the objective of the structural refis and asset portfolios will be to provide liquidity to the system in order to not rely entirely on one-week and three-month operations. Hence, the relative sizes of these three tools for injecting liquidity will be determined mainly by operational considerations, rather than to affect the policy stance.

For legal reasons, we suspect the APP and PEPP portfolios will be run down to zero and, if needed, a new structural asset portfolio built up from scratch. This is cleaner from a legal perspective given the difference in objectives, even if it would not differ substantially from a simple relabelling. At the same time, any structural asset portfolio would likely have a different maturity structure from APP and PEPP, and it could pursue some “green<sup>”</sup> objectives. Similarly, the longerterm refis may also pursue “green<sup>”</sup> objectives. And, they may differ from the fixed-rate full allotment at the regular oneweek and three-month operations, for example by offering only fixed amounts of reserves. In this way, the ECB would

control the sizes of the longer-term refis and of its asset portfolio. The exact maturity of the longer-term refinancing operations would also likely take into account existing bank liquidity regulations.

When will decisions about the longer-term refinancing operations and the structural asset portfolio be made? The ECB has announced a review of the system in 2026. It may at that point think about the form and sizes of these two liquidityproviding mechanisms. But, this could also happen later: it will depend on the urgency at the time, i.e. by how large excess liquidity still is and when the ECB expects overnight rates to begin to drift away from the deposit rate.

## Two decisions that were not taken

Ahead of the announcement, there was a lot of interest in whether the ECB would raise the reserve requirement for banks from 1% to 2%. As it currently pays zero interest on these, it would have imposed a cost on the banks, whilst reducing the ECB<sup>’</sup>s own interest expense. In the end, the ECB left the reserve ratio unchanged, presumably because it is comfortable with the cash flow implication for its own balance sheet. This suggests that an increase is now unlikely also in the foreseeable future.

Second, no mention was made to giving nonbanks access to the deposit facility. This might have tackled the “leaky floor<sup>”</sup> problem as transactions between banks and nonbanks in the overnight market occur below the deposit rate given that nonbanks do not have the ability to “park<sup>”</sup> reserves with the ECB at the deposit rate. However, the extent of this has not been quite modest and quite steady over time (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Spread between €str and ECB deposit rate
![](images/2215d466e6720b803c8a60f1f94f94aad961d6fa0267f033dc98e6ca68a8abaf.jpg)

## A pragmatic and flexible choice

Overall, the ECB has concluded its operational framework review by making only small changes. In addition, it has signalled that parameters can be changed again in future, should this be necessary, whilst the size and composition of any LTROs and structural bond portfolios will be decided only when they are actually needed.

## A BoE watcher’s practical guide to rates

Purely data driven models such as a Taylor Rule can offer a steer on central bank rates over time

• But in real time there is too much uncertainty about the data and what is happing in the macro economy

• We take a more practical approach for the purpose of modelling short term MPC rate decisions...

• ...also incorporating information about the BoE<sup>’</sup>s reaction function, voting behavior and global rates

Interest rates are determined by many factors. Ultimately, a central bank is constrained by its own mandate and it is this combined with both cyclical and structural macro economic developments that dictate where the policy rate ends up. Over shorter term horizons, however, there is significant real time uncertainty about what is actually happening in the economy. Even with a very detailed parsing of the data each month, different people can place different weights on various elements of the data and end up with entirely different views.

This is evident in the three way vote split on the MPC at the moment. While the true picture will emerge eventually, central bank decisions at the next meeting often come down to the informed judgements of individuals. This is why so much weight is placed on central bank speak and forward guidance in the near term, and why such guidance has a short shelf life.

In this note we present an empirical analysis supporting this conclusion. We use a Taylor Rule to show how macroeconomic developments determine where the level of rates ends up through time, and an econometric model of the change in rates (Figure 1) to illustrate the best real time predictors of near term policy decisions. In addition to the role played by the data, this analysis highlights the importance of central bank communications and shifts in its reaction function. We conclude by sketching out what we would expect to see happen this year in order for the BoE to cut, stay on hold and hike. The practical takeaways from this analysis are as follows:

1. Rate changes often come in clusters or cycles. Once the BoE does eventually start moving, it often continues

2. Dissenting votes in sufficient size can have leading properties about future decisions

3. Rate changes are more likely at forecast meetings

4. The actions of major central banks correlate very well with BoE decisions, although the relationship is not nec-

essarily causal

5. Speeches reflect the mood, but offer limited predictive power on future decisions

6. Carefully watch the monthly data, but be prepared in case the BoE views the same data through a different lens

Figure 1: Model of the change in BoE policy rate
![](images/d0bf1335dbf29d6697124839cc7d355777edc42628c3297d5bf367f484a4113b.jpg)

## Macro factors determine rates over time

Over the long run, real interest rates will be determined purely by structural factors such as demographics, debt dynamics and productivity trends. These influence the interaction of investment and saving in an economy, which determines equilibrium interest rates. As a small open economy, the UK will be significantly affected by how these factors influence global rates. Central banks have no direct control over the equilibrium, and are primarily trying to manage cyclical developments. Over the course of an average business cycle, rates will therefore tend to average close to their equilibrium.

Over a more medium term horizon, i.e. within the business cycle, the broad movements in interest rates are also dictated by the data. While there is uncertainty about what is happening in real time, sustained deviations in inflation or spare capacity away from equilibrium levels are ultimately likely to prompt a change in rates. This is consistent with central bank mandates that typically revolve around inflation with an objective for optimal growth or labour market outturns.

These medium to long term features determining the central bank<sup>’</sup>s policy rate can be illustrated by a simple Taylor Rule. This data driven model uses the benefit of hindsight, however, as not all inputs are known in real time. We prefer the Balanced Taylor Rule presented here for the UK, following the specification shown below and as illustrated in Figure 2. We assume a real equilibrium interest rate (r \*) of 0.5% since 2009, and proxy the output gap by using the unemployment rate. We use an Okun coefficient of two and a time varying NAIRU (ur<sup>\*</sup>) derived from a filtering of the unemployment rate:

$$
\mathrm { r _ { t } } { = } \mathrm { r _ { t } } ^ { \ast } + \pi ^ { \ast } + 0 . 5 ( \pi _ { \mathrm { t } } - \pi ^ { \ast } ) + ( \mathrm { u r _ { t } } - \mathrm { u r _ { t } } ^ { \ast } ) ^ { \ast } - 2
$$

![](images/219eb83c7be89bf91ddfaaa8206c246e941260516e9a70f343a9494ee2eb6006.jpg)

## But with plenty of short run deviation

Over the short run, rates set by the central bank often deviate significantly from simple Taylor Rule estimates. There are several explanations for this:

Unobservable inputs and data mis-measurement. The true equilibrium levels of the unemployment rate and interest rates can only be inferred after the event at best, whereas central banks have to make judgements in real time. The data in general can also be heavily revised and complicate the BoE<sup>’</sup>s real time understanding, as can be seen in the official labour market data in the UK recently. As such, Taylor Rules benefit from hindsight.

Forecast errors. Rates affect inflation with a lag. The central bank will want to be forward looking, and might believe that a sudden move in the data will not be sustained. But this relies on judgement and accurate forecasting, which can have significant errors. Sustained moves in the data can force the central bank to deviate from its initial prior, and follow what a simple data driven model would have suggested all along.

The effective lower bound. On several occasions after the GFC, the effective lower bound (ELB) on rates prevented central banks from easing as much as they might have liked. This led to the use of other tools such as QE, and explains some of the large gaps shown in Figure 2 when the data effectively called for significantly negative rates.

Risk management considerations. Central banks often choose to deviate from rule based models on the grounds of managing risks. This ties in with the point made about the ELB above, where concerns about the efficacy of alternative tools such as QE led to more downside risk management by the BoE after the GFC. The BoE also tightened cautiously in 2022/23 due to concerns about a potential gas induced recession.

## Modeling short run BoE decisions

Given these challenges, we have modelled changes in the BoE<sup>’</sup>s policy rate from one meeting to the next using more than just data. We incorporate a range of variables encapsulating the BoE<sup>’</sup>s reaction function and other influences. We can explain around two thirds of the one meeting ahead change in the BoE<sup>’</sup>s policy rate over this period. The results are described in more detail in the Table 1, and its footnote. But here we draw these main conclusions:

Strong inertial properties. The best predictor of the next decision in interest rates is what happened at the prior meeting. This may seem obvious, but this important observation contrasts with often quite choppy movements in the data. Once the BoE gets going, it quite often continues in a rate cycle. Our model implies that a 25bp cut at the previous meeting is worth another 10-20bps at the next meeting, on average.

Forecast rounds matter. Most BoE observers recognize that changes in interest rates are more likely to occur alongside quarterly forecast rounds, i.e. at four of the eight meetings scheduled for the year. If there is little urgency for an imminent change, the BoE is more likely wait for a forecast meeting . This enables it to better communicate its decision, as there are no press conferences held at the other meetings. For this reason, some of the model inputs we use take on added significance when they coincide with a forecast round. More recently, forecast meetings may matter a little less than before: the current calendar configuration means there is often more data available in the run up to non-forecast meetings.

Information content from other central banks. There is often a close correlation between BoE rates and movements from the ECB and Fed. Many draw the conclusion that this is causal. There are indeed some direct effects, via the currency and a potential tendency for groupthink among the central banking community. But the high comovement in rates likely has a lot to do with the strong cross country correlation in the data themselves, and the way in which central banks collectively interpret and forecast from these developments in real time. The recent actions of other central banks can sometimes act as a good proxy for what the BoE will be inclined to do at the next meeting. Our model suggests that a 25bp move from other central banks such as the Fed, in isolation, is worth 2-7bps on the BoE<sup>’</sup>s next rate decision.

A complex relationship with the data. All central banks are data dependent, but it is often hard to represent the complexity of all the factors being considered in real time. The focus can shift from one period to the next, with services inflation under particular scrutiny at the moment as a guide to where the CPI will settle after its decline. Data surprises may matter more than outturns per se, although it is hard to quantity this effect accurately through time. These representation issues also likely help to explain the significance of other central bank actions and past BoE

Impact shown in basis points

decisions, as described above. That said, our model suggests that each 1%-pt rise in the Taylor Rule as described earlier is worth 2bps in terms of predicting the outcome of the next BoE meeting. A one point rise in the PMI is worth 1bp. These estimates are small in any individual month, but if sustained their impact grows over time due to the lag structure used in the modelling.

Dissents matter more in size and when sustained. The BoE<sup>’</sup>s MPC has nine voting members with decisions determined by majority. Early votes on rates by some members - i.e. a dissent from the majority decision - offer some information content on how the majority might vote at subsequent meetings. Each dissent on its own is worth around 3bps of predictive power for the next MPC meeting. But the relevance of dissents increases with their number and the length of time they are sustained for.

Speeches are often more reflective than predictive. JPMorgan produces a hawk-dove score using natural language processing of central banks speeches. This variable is not significant in the model we use. One reason might be that MPC speeches are often used to explain BoE decisions, rather than front-running them. But other variables in the model also likely capture some of the same information. There are some notable exceptions, for example if the Governor were to use a speech to steer expectations. But overall we find there is more predictive power in MPC dissents rather than speeches per se.

So ΔBoE rates = β ΔBoE rates + β ΔBoE rates \* MPR dummy + β ΔBoE rates + β Net hawkish dissents etc. A dummy variable is interacted with these variables to capture the added importance of forecast (MPR) meetings. Adj. R squared is 0.62, Standard Error of 11bps, DW-stat 2.04. All variables significant with 95% confidence except the second lag on rates (93%)

## Applying the model to the current debate

Table 2 below shows the model<sup>’</sup>s prediction for rates based on a set of assumptions we have made about what will happen this year. The results are therefore highly conditional on those assumptions. For the period beyond the next meeting, the model relies partly on its own past prediction of the policy rate in order to project forward. This serves to make the forecast from one meeting to the next a little smoother than the

MPC operates in practice, which is to make more discreet policy changes usually in multiples of 25bps. But this property in the model is not that different from how markets themselves tend to price the BoE several months ahead. The number of net hawkish dissents is itself dependent on the path of rates through time, and requires some subjective judgement in order to make projections beyond the very next meeting.

We assume that both of the MPC<sup>’</sup>s hawkish dissents disappear by the June meeting. We also assume the Fed starts cutting in June, and then eases a further two times this year. Our Taylor Rule estimate comes off by 300bps in the second quarter due to the expected large drop in inflation. We also assume the PMI will lose around seven points in 2H24 owing to our forecast that growth will return to stagnation following a temporary 1H24 boost.

Based on our assumptions, the most likely months for a cut this year according the model are August and November. But June, September and December are all close candidates, partly because of the pattern of our recently revised Fed call that has three cuts in for these months. These assumptions are consistent with a cumulative 45bps of easing by year end, a bit less than the 65bps priced by markets at the time of writing. Prior to the recent change in our Fed call, when there were five consecutive cuts in the forecast, the model was projective cumulative easing from the BoE of 67bps. Our forecast assumes August for the first cut, and has a cumulative easing of 75bps for this year.

To illustrate how quickly things could change, we consider the following alternative scenario. The Fed instead starts cutting in September, hawkish dissents remain until September, and the PMI remains steady at current levels. This would dramatically reduce predicted cuts to a cumulative 4bps by year end, suggesting the BoE would be close to holding rates.

In order to deliver a 25bp hike this year, hawkish dissents would need to remain with one additional member joining the group by September. The Fed would need to at least go on hold, with the PMI remaining unchanged at current levels and the unemployment rate falling a further 0.5%-pts.

## India’s Household Consumption Survey: 5 takeaways

• Survey reveals per-capita consumption growth has softened over the last decade

• The gap between rural and urban consumption narrows and urban inequality falls...

• ...though these results are at odds with high frequency data post-pandemic

• CPI could be potentially lower by 30-50 bp based on updated consumption patterns but caveats apply

After a gap of 11 years, India<sup>’</sup>s National Statistics Office (NSO) has released a Household Consumption Expenditure Survey (HCES), capturing detailed household consumption patterns. The Survey was conducted between August 2022 and July 2023 (referred to as HCES, 2023). Authorities have released the Survey<sup>’</sup>s “Fact Sheet<sup>”</sup> for now, with detailed results, including unit-level data, expected to be released in the coming months. Here we highlight the key takeaways from the fact sheet.

Before getting to the key results, however, some housekeeping is in order. The current HCES (2023) has undergone various methodological changes (see the Appendix below) and hence the results are not strictly comparable with prior surveys. Furthermore, the survey period of 2022-23 suggests consumption that year was likely still impacted by potential hysteresis from the pandemic. Authorities are therefore conducting another survey (August 2023-July 2024) which should be a better representation of the post-pandemic equilibrium (HCES, 2024). Ideally, therefore, both surveys should be read together for making sense of consumption trends.

## Takeaway #1: Real Consumption growth has softened over the last decade

Across the last 11 years, real per-capita consumption growth was relatively soft growing at a compound annual growth rate (CAGR) of 3.2% in rural areas and 2.6% in urban areas. In the previous surveys, between 2005 and 2012, this growth was 4.5% and 4.3% in rural and urban areas respectively (Figure 1). Recall that the post pandemic recovery has been characterized by sluggish consumption growth, which has likely weighed on these findings. The bigger picture emanating from the Survey though is that consumption has slowed over the last decade vis-à-vis the 2005-12 period though still higher than the 2000-2005 period, during which real, per-capita consumption barely grew.

Figure 1: Real Consumption Per Capita
![](images/aa4839ae0c54c952c0cd699c07563a9882413d498b6017af1286ed285fe7bcc6.jpg)

Growth of private consumption per-capita in the national accounts has been reported at 4.6% for the last 11 years, higher than the survey results. However, there have always been some differences between National Accounts and Survey data due to methodological issues – differences in consumer basket, computational methods, and consumer base – because of which they are not strictly comparable. For instance in the previous period between 2005 and 2012 private consumption per-capita growth was 6.7% higher than survey<sup>’</sup>s 4.3-4.5%.

## Takeaway #2: The Rural-Urban Gap Continues to Narrow

The gap between rural and urban consumption levels began narrowing from the 2004-5 survey, and that narrowing has gathered pace over the last decade, according to the latest survey. In particular, rural per-capita consumption as a percent of urban inched up from 52% to 54% between 2005 and 2012 (7-year period) and then the share increased from 54% to 58% between 2012 and 2023 (11-year period). On an average annual basis, therefore, the gap between urban and rural narrowed by about 0.3% per year between 2005 and 2012 and by about 0.4% in the latest survey period (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Average Annual Reduction in Rural-Urban GAP
![](images/5bfb297441528ad12fa944efba496b359a5bf8497388f0cfd76996634b2e3f6a.jpg)

## Takeaway #3: Urban inequality falls sharply

The Survey<sup>’</sup>s factsheet provides a distribution of rural and urban per-capita expenditure across fractile classes which can used to compute inequality. What does the Gini Coefficient – a widely-used measure for computing inequality with a higher coefficient representing increased inequality – reveal? It reveals that there has been meaningful drop in urban inequality over the last 11 years with the Gini coefficient reducing from 0.37 in 2012 to 0.31 in 2023. The rural economy reveals a different picture, with the Gini coefficient reducing only very modestly from 0.28 in 2012 to 0.27 in 2023 (Figure 3). In contrast, between 2005 and 2012 there was a slight increase in Gini coefficient across both rural and urban population.

Figure 3: Gini Coefficient
![](images/eef28d7b96c9fb6c6dc03bfc97ddeed2fff964be763140884c30eade4d317e71.jpg)
Source: HCES Surveys, J.P. Morgan

In sum, therefore, over the last 11 years, rural consumption has been catching up with urban, but inequality trends within urban and rural are very different, with urban inequality declining much faster than rural inequality.

Prima facie, the second and third takeaways of the Survey (rural catching up with urban and urban inequality declining) are at odds with several proxy variables since the pandemic (wage growth, auto sales, durables versus FMCG goods, housing data) which suggest the opposite. In theory, it<sup>’</sup>s possible there is no inconsistency because the survey is conducted across a 11-year period while the pandemic is just 4 years old. So, it<sup>’</sup>s possible the survey<sup>’</sup>s trends reveal pre-pandemic dynamics, partially undone by the post-pandemic period. From this perspective, what the next HCES (2024) suggests will be very revealing.

## Takeaway #4: CPI weight changes to have inflation impact

Apart from documenting changing consumption patterns, the Consumer Expenditure Surveys matter because they are used in determining the weights of the CPI inflation basket. Currents CPI weights are from the 2011-12 HCES.

So how have consumption patterns changed in the latest survey? As income levels rise, expenditure on food is expected to fall while certain non-food categories (durables and services) are expected to increase. More generally, “superior<sup>”</sup> goods are expected to benefit at the expense of “inferior<sup>”</sup> goods as

income levels rise. The survey reports that the weight of food has gone down, with the share of food in the consumption basket declining from 52.9% in 2012 to 46.4% in 2023 in rural areas and from 42.6% to 39.2% in urban areas (Figure 4). In contrast, the share of services, including health and education, has increased from 19.8% to 24.9% in rural areas and from 24.3% to 32.4% in urban areas.

Figure 4: Share of Food in MPCE
![](images/987a49b43b080a46af831da90aa5507e679ba6cab55694574b31159a2f9004b9.jpg)
Source: HCES (2022-23) Factsheet, J.P. Morgan

Weights in the CPI basket are only changed after two consecutive surveys, and therefore results of the 2023-24 survey (due to be completed in July 2024) are awaited. As a thought experiment, however, how much would CPI inflation change if the basket reflects the HCES, 2023 results? CPI inflation in FY24 (average 5.4% oya) could have been potentially lower by 30-50 bps due to lower weight of food in the consumer basket (particularly cereals wherein inflation averaged 11%).

## Takeaway #5: Puzzling regressive rural transfers

To improve the computational accuracy, the latest HCES also estimates the imputed value of cumulative governmental transfers received by households free of cost through various welfare programs. This is additional to a typical Monthly Per Capita Consumption Expenditure (MPCE) computed based on past practice. Comparing two estimates of MPCE across fractile class reveals an interesting contrast.

• Transfers are progressive in urban areas; they decline as one moves up the consumption deciles.

Transfers are partially regressive in rural areas; they increase (albeit modestly) as one moves up the consumption decile (until the 70-80% decile) before declining (Figure 5).

Figure 5: Value of Government Transferred Free Items

Rs. per household per month

![](images/9da00e0c6bf65e9294fe254a857fb03e2a548234228b85fe6ebdbeedd62f390d.jpg)
Source: HCES (2022-23) Factsheet, J.P. Morgan

To be sure this is just based on the fact sheet. The details of the survey should throw more light on this puzzle.

## Appendix: Methodological changes

The HCES: 2022-23 has undergone important methodological changes vis-à-vis previous surveys. Changes have been introduced to improve the quality of survey results. Consequently, HCES 2022-2023 is not strictly comparable with the past. Major changes include:

Item Coverage: Items covered in the questionnaire have increased to 405 from 347 in the previous survey. This increase is understandable to reflect changes in consumption patterns across the time.

Change in Questionnaire: HCES uses three separate questionnaires compared to a single questionnaire in the past. This allows enhanced capturing of information, visa-vis one long questionnaire which could lead to fatigue on the part of the respondent.

Multiple Visits for data collection: In a break from the usual practice of a single visit, three surveys questionnaires are used across three separate monthly visits in a quarter.

## Philippines: A primer on the BSP

• Policy rate is driven by inflation and FX; Fed may matter less as financial flows become less rate-sensitive

BSP intervention aims to curb volatility; BOP fundamentals still the primary driver of PHP performance

Foreign flows drive balance sheet activity, system liquidity and demand for onshore securities

In this note, we aim to provide a comprehensive primer on the BSP<sup>’</sup>s major policy frameworks, encompassing monetary policy, the exchange rate, and the balance sheet. Our research shows that the policy rate reaction function is driven by inflation and FX, to the extent that the latter affects the former via imported inflation. The Fed was a significant driver of rates in the 2000s but has become less important as the BOP shifts towards long-term and more stable financing sources such as FDI and official loans.

The BSP<sup>’</sup>s intervention aims to curb volatility and still allows for market forces to drive FX. As such, our constructive BOP outlook, if it materializes, should translate into PHP strength. Relative outperformance is also in the cards, as the BSP faces less pressure to rebuild reserves vis-à-vis regional central banks due to strong buffers. Finally, balance sheet activity mostly reflects foreign reserve changes. This means that foreign flows, and not domestic credit creation, drive system liquidity and provide the marginal demand for local securities.
