## All sensitivities lead to inflation

Like most central banks in the region, the BSP has a price stability mandate and has adopted an inflation targeting regime since 2002. Our Taylor Rule model (Table 1) shows that while headline CPI was not a statistically significant explanatory variable in the BSP<sup>’</sup>s reaction function during the pre-GFC period, it has become a key driver of policy rate deci-

sions since 2010 as policy-makers gradually fine-tuned the inflation target (Figure 1) to be better anchored for a mediumterm policy horizon.

Figure 1: Philippines policy rate vs. inflation %oya
![](images/5b3215824fd6e0052479781260ff13a1756de5199ef4dc0cf8b0cd4c50dadfa8.jpg)

The policy rate is also historically sensitive to external balances/FX, as proxied by net reserves. This is not surprising, given the significant role of the trade-weighted exchange rate (NEER) in domestic inflation dynamics (Table 2). While the exchange rate pass-through (ERPT) may have declined in tandem with inflation persistence in the past two decades, it is still subject to periodic, non-linear spikes during commodity price shocks, most recently in 2021-22. This applies particularly to major net commodity importers such as the Philippines (such imports account for around 7% of GDP).

Table 2: Philippines headline inflation (%oya)
<table><tr><td>Independent variable (%oya)</td><td>Coefficient</td><td>P-val</td></tr><tr><td>Constant</td><td>2.57</td><td>0.00</td></tr><tr><td>Brent crude oil price (local currency)</td><td>0.01</td><td>0.00</td></tr><tr><td>World food price, 12M adv (local currency)</td><td>0.05</td><td>0.00</td></tr><tr><td>NEER, 6M adv</td><td>(0.15)</td><td>0.00</td></tr><tr><td>R-squared</td><td>F-stat</td><td>0.00</td></tr><tr><td>SER</td><td>Observations</td><td>84</td></tr></table>

Dependent variable: headline CPI, Source: J.P. Morgan estimate

Figure 2: Philippines trade balance vs. fiscal balance % of GDP
![](images/a017937ce5605dba4ff9dcf86adc43fedb9b881071568c2a95db1e24be28f850.jpg)
1. Denoted in SITC 0-4 categories 2. Denoted in SITC 5-8 categories

In addition to commodity-driven terms-of-trade shocks, BOP/ FX pressures have also come from stronger domestic demand. The implementation of an infrastructure-led capex cycle during the 2016-19 period led to a material deterioration in trade and fiscal balances (Figure 2), resulting in FX

depreciation. In response, the BSP hiked the policy rate by 175bps in 2018 with the aim of slowing down economic activity via tighter credit conditions and import compression (Figure 3).

Figure 3: BSP policy rate vs. raw material & capital goods import
![](images/a85fdc472fbe5ce4a9ed9ef92f9ef57497fa478178b4f5ee3b6f64e2905a4e32.jpg)

Global interest rates (proxied by the Fed funds rate) also feature prominently in the BSP<sup>’</sup>s reaction function, consistent with the high-yield/twin-deficit archetype. However, the significance of the Fed funds rate as a policy driver has declined over time (i.e., higher p-value, lower R squared in Table 1) as the correlation between the absolute levels of the Fed funds rate/BSP-Fed policy rate differential and the financial account/NEER weakened (Figure 4 and 5).

Figure 4: Interest rates vs. Philippines financial account
![](images/ca2745de3d6b86d52e95e0d1d242bab6a7057b13b10055799c0437252a54a562.jpg)

Figure 5: Interest rates vs. PHP NEER
![](images/73a22a4b1fba1239adde24eea9959031badd28475d678775d6749837608b419a.jpg)

This is largely because the Philippines financial account has become less dependent on volatile, rate-sensitive portfolio inflows. Instead, FDI and official loans (via the other investment account) have played a more important role in financing current account deficits in the past decade (Figure 6).

Figure 6: Philippines financial account inflows % of GDP
![](images/b928fcfe571b6120b14590d1737e1414850c241760e5dbbdb4a0ed5e73b8d306.jpg)

The key question is if the compositional shift towards more stable, long-term, and less rate-sensitive financial flows is sustainable. If so, then there is a case to be made that the BSP may pay less attention to global interest rates going forward. For now, we expect the BSP<sup>’</sup>s reaction function to remain sensitive to both domestic (e.g., administrative price adjustments, public capex) and external (e.g., commodity price, current account) factors that have a material impact on headline inflation.

## Asymmetric but effective

Although the Taylor Rule shows that headline CPI leads the policy rate by three quarters in the past 15 years, the implementation of the inflation targeting regime is asymmetric. Above-target inflation systematically leads to policy tightening (e.g., May 2018, May 2022), but below-target inflation does not necessarily result in easing. Instead, rate cut decisions are discretionary, triggered by either sustained disinflation (e.g., June 2016, May 2019) or exogenous shocks (e.g., February 2020), and independent of where headline CPI is relative to target.

As such, anticipating the timing and magnitude of the upcom ing policy easing cycle is not straightforward. Despite CPI returning to the 2%-4% target in recent months, the BSP remains wary of several factors (e.g., base effects in mid-2024, rice prices, administrative price adjustments) that could complicate the disinflation path in 2024. There is also the added uncertainty of when and how much the Fed can ease. Nonetheless, we continue to expect the BSP to deliver 100bps of rate cuts in 2H24, premised on sustained disinflation and the Fed starting an easing cycle in mid-2024. Given the historically elevated ex-ante real policy rate (Figure 7), additional easing in 2025 is also in the cards.

Figure 7: Philippines policy rate vs. inflation
![](images/81923e42a8745be4f428174eb56fc2c80691489f53c5ea3bf179119e16ad17e7.jpg)

We also find that monetary policy transmission (Figure 8 and 9) has improved significantly since the adoption of the 2%-4% inflation target in 2015. This is a result of incremental reforms in monetary policy operations, from expanding the list of eligible participants to allowing market-driven pricing of OMO instruments.

Figure 8: Philippines policy rate vs. money market rate
![](images/578b3d12ab62775c5d52a765ed77e3df10d71f70a9933383841f7e921ed29d67.jpg)

Figure 9: Philippines policy rate vs. bank rates % p.a.
![](images/08e73b1cd8996aff66abfc889208e540181782070a3b33f28e60c1228a899e17.jpg)

## Taking the foot off the (intervention) pedal

BSP<sup>’</sup>s FX policy has always aimed to manage volatility instead of targeting specific levels. Since 2010, changes in reserve assets (proxy of valuation-adjusted FX buying or selling) have perfectly mirrored changes in the BOP (Figure 10), suggesting limited intervention to manage volatility while still allowing market forces to drive the exchange rate (Figure 11). Even when the BSP verbally signaled intervention during the recent bout of volatility in 3Q23, we did not find evidence of outsized dollar selling relative to BOP outflows. As a result, the peso has gradually moved towards fair value levels according to inflation-adjusted valuation models (Figure 12).

Figure 10: Philippines BOP balance vs. change in reserve assets
![](images/32fe4d3c946afcc6f3fc29947fbcde0c79110039f6e6288d799704dc54c557f3.jpg)

Figure 11: Philippines BOP vs. USDPHP
![](images/95536c88df24bc23628e68da22c9f28f8f526680114427f342f2f2a37253118d.jpg)

Figure 12: Philippines NEER vs. REER
![](images/a075c243d3e5ed97d49b5f8bb17f13c0ec2956488e39d1a7a317c4dea1cf3611.jpg)

The implication of limited intervention is a BSP that could be tolerant of PHP appreciation later this year, if our constructive BOP outlook (led by CA deficit narrowing and resilient nonportfolio financial inflows) materializes.

There is also a case for relative currency outperformance against regional peers. Using the IMF<sup>’</sup>s Assessing Reserve Adequacy (ARA) methodology, we find that the Philippines has one of the highest reserve buffers in Asia (Table 3). All else equal, the BSP does not have as much pressure as other regional central banks to accumulate reserves amidst prospective Fed rate cuts and easier external conditions in 2H24.

## BSP balance sheet – (usually) a quiet place

In the past decade, the BSP<sup>’</sup>s balance sheet activity has an outsized influence on excess liquidity in the domestic banking system (Figure 13). However, apart from the bond purchase program during the pandemic, the central bank has always shown restraint in injecting liquidity via net domestic asset (NDA) creation. Instead, balance sheet expansions are driven primarily by reserve accumulation (Figure 14) which is then sterilized (Figure 15) to manage money supply and inflationary pressures, and vice versa.

From an investment perspective, what this means is that foreign inflows, especially via the current account (e.g., exports, remittance), are pivotal in driving excess liquidity, and by extension, the marginal demand for onshore securities.

Figure 13: BSP total assets vs. banking system excess liquidity
![](images/aa1b1d4f4af7b2d274efe4787119ec1ab2c446a3854844c98d6b77edf4f0ccdb.jpg)

Figure 14: BSP balance sheet
![](images/40491a944c3a745cc03e3cf0e55432ecbfb6dff981f492803a757434c81cee0a.jpg)
Source: CEIC, J.P. Morgan estimates

Figure 15: BSP net foreign assets (NFA) vs. sterilization activity
![](images/d63e8c576e8ea1220582d48b22e73f14df449ef2f4b9aa2d744c1d49bd6b89e2.jpg)

## Less is more

In many respects, the BSP<sup>’</sup>s policy-making has become more orthodox, streamlined, and effective over time. Monetary policy is singularly focused on inflation and its various drivers. Currency interventions aim to manage volatility without distorting fundamentals. Discretionary balance sheet activity is kept to a minimum to manage onshore liquidity. This creates a more predictable policy-making environment, which allows fundamentals (e.g., inflation, BOP) to drive asset prices (e.g., bonds, FX).

## Kenya: Shifting winds

• The IMF supported Kenya<sup>’</sup>s shift away from a loose macro-economic policy stance

• This pivot is evident in commitment to fiscal restraint, reducing external vulnerabilities

Tighter monetary policy should help keep the CAD below 5% of GDP, containing pressures on FX reserves

Kenya will probably meet external funding needs, with the next big amortisations in 2027-2028

In our 2023 framework note we posited that limited international market access amid wider twin imbalances would test Kenya<sup>’</sup>s macro resilience. The fiscal deficit remained high at 5.6% of GDP in FY22/23 (ending July), albeit narrowing from 6.2% in FY20/21, while the current account deficit (CAD) stood above 5%. As a result, failing FX reserves raised concerns on the ability to meet its large hard currency redemption profile in 2024, with the country typically relying on foreign debt to meet balance of payment (BoP) needs. In particular, Kenya needed to refinance the \$2bn eurobond in June 2024, and had large public investment and energy imports needs which weighed on the BoP.

While the macro-economic situation remained challenging, substantial policy shifts are underway and at least parts of these are likely longer lasting. Kenyan authorities significantly cut public capex and introduced revenue measures, which helped narrow CAD and supported fiscal balance. Moreover, monetary policy tightened markedly, and dollar liquidity also improved amid strong IMF/WB support and partially regained market access. We see risks that the thawing liquidity and a stable KES environment incentivise the restart of the country<sup>’</sup>s investment-led growth agenda. Yet, the commitment to IMF-led fiscal consolidation, front-loaded policy rate hikes in 2H23, and likely sustained higher real rates, should cap domestic absorption. We argue in this note that the CAD likely stays below 5% of GDP to curb pressure on FX buffers.

## Narrower CAD thanks to lower imports

The most visible manifestation of Kenya<sup>’</sup>s macro-economic adjustment under the IMF-funded programme since 2021 is the compression of external imbalances. From \$6.0bn (5.7% of GDP) at the end of 2021, the CAD compressed significantly to \$4.3bn (4% of GDP) by end-2023. The goods and services deficit narrowed due to import compression while exports languished despite a gentle rebound in tourism buoying the services balance (Figure 1). Meanwhile, the income balance remained robust at around \$5bn on solid growth in remittances.

Figure 1: Kenya current account dynamics
![](images/3c2a3fc2100c509edbfd8f864865c3b0c3db77ec6105a29ee4d41806b357666f.jpg)

We trace the fall in imports largely to lower government investment amid limited financing sources. Public sector capex reduced by 1.4%pt to 3.5% of GDP as at end of FY22/23, underpinning the bulk of the narrowing in the headline fiscal deficit to 5.6% in FY22/23 (ending in June). Yet, current spending has remained broadly flat at around 19% of GDP, underscoring the potentially temporary cyclical nature of the fiscal consolidation path from the expenditure side. This mirrors the public sector CAD (S-I) narrowing from 7.6% of GDP to 5.6% in 2023, reflecting a combination of a public investment slowing from 5.3% of GDP in 2021 to 4.1%, and tax initiatives (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Kenya savings-investment gap
![](images/445b072175e9a43e3b5bfd44955679070d60c77c86cddc5b2e6718eede72fda6.jpg)

At the same time, the private sector runs a positive S-I gap, but the surplus compressed from 2.1% of GDP to 1.5% in 2023 as savings fell (3.1%pts of GDP to 14.9%) more than investment (2.5%pts to 13.4%) since 2021. All told, with capex shipments accounting for a third of total imports it is not surprising to see the decline in capital imports leading the improvement in CAD since 2021.

The IMF expects the public sector S-I to turn positive by 2027 as public investment is capped at 5.6% of GDP, which in our view is an ambitious target. Indeed, the government<sup>’</sup>s 2030 economic agenda envisions investment climbing 10%-pts of GDP to 30%, in part to build climate buffers and continue the infrastructure-led growth framework. The expectation is that the private sector will lead the investment recovery with a rise to 14.6% of GDP (from 13.4) by 2027. Yet, efforts are underway to source more bilateral loans to revitalise stalled road projects point to likely higher public sector

investment, which may exert upward pressure on CAD over the medium term. That said, our baseline sees the public sector S-I settling at around 5% of GDP over the medium term. This is consistent with our headline fiscal deficit forecasts of 5.5% of GDP in FY23/24 (ending this June) and 4.5% in FY24/25 as the IMF programme underpins continued fiscal discipline.

Exports more sensitive to global demand Kenya<sup>’</sup>s exports are mostly sensitive to global growth and US, EA, UAE consumer dynamics given the high dependence on tourism and horticulture. Our estimated coefficient of external demand suggests that a 1% increase in external demand would increase exports by 1.8%pt of GDP. Meanwhile, the price elasticity is neither statistically nor economically significant, with a 1% increase in Kenya<sup>’</sup>s REER resulting in an exports volume increase of 0.2%.

Figure 3: Kenya exports elasticities
![](images/aae505c77eb796ab44b6ce3e0dd8ba7d3fcb9405c969b4d17c7c0668ca8250e4.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan

These results are not inconsistent with the events over the last two years. Even though the price elasticity is small, the 25% depreciation against the US dollar and the 10% real exchange rate depreciation should have helped avoid the 0.2%y/y exports decline last year. Instead, only net travel benefitted as the weaker KES inspired increased tourism activity. This is unsurprising because services exports are found to have the greatest “demand elasticity<sup>”</sup>, with travel accounting for c.82% of total services exports (Figure 3). Based on this, external balances remain exposed to a deterioration in global demand, the KES correction (appreciation), as well as improved consumer purchasing power.

## Outlook: Monetary policy joins the party

GDP growth remained solid at an estimated 5.4%y/y increase (vs. 5% potential growth) last year even as monetary conditions tightened (Figure 4). This is despite the CBK mounting real rates to a 2017 high of c.600bp by December 2023. We trace much of the gains in GDP from the supply side as a fading drought buoyed agriculture output (the sector contributes 25% to GDP, and employs c.60% of the total labour force). As the impact fades and the delayed monetary pass-through kicks in, we look for a small moderation in GDP growth to 5.2%-5.3% this and next year.

Figure 4: Real monetary conditions index index, 2019=100
![](images/7a001327a85bcf7057d7881a07a1c54674e3fa7ff15a2cfc0a7810ee53257384.jpg)
Source: CBK, Haver Analytics, J.P. Morgan

In addition, a potential shift in the reaction function took place at the CBK following changes in personnel. The CBK has been intent on minimising FX interventions, allowing the KES to depreciate significantly and assume an increased role as a shock absorber. The CBK recently sold dollars amid heightened volatility, but we think future interventions will be guarded, in line with the IT framework put in motion in 3Q23.

![](images/26c2a7b8f266ced2229be010b1625b3f3917a59481a7522d5f0714f109b0dd40.jpg)

Overall, we see a controlled CAD expansion to 4.7% of GDP by end-2025, higher than the 3.2% we think supports FX accumulation, but below the over 5% historical mean (Figure 5). Sustaining the CAD at this level amid a return of portfolio investors and continued multilateral support should fade refinancing concerns. Indeed, near-term external debt servicing pressures abated after the \$1.5bn Eurobond buyback in February. Yet, Kenya will have to redeem \$1bn Eurobonds on average in 2027 and 2028. Efforts to avert external balances worsening excessively and maintaining policy reforms to keep the multilateral “confidence anchor<sup>”</sup> will therefore be important. If so, we believe Kenya will meet the external debt obligations, with the authorities also seeking alternative funding sources such asSamurai and green bonds. Yet, investors risk perception for Kenya could resurface if the consolidation path is derailed post the IMF programme expiration in 1H25.

## Nigeria: Monetary tightening needs a fiscal counterpart

• Monetary policy adjustments are underway but policy mix with tighter fiscal stance likely necessary

Government expenditure to remain around 9.6% of GDP this year on higher debt service costs and wage hikes

• Oil revenue accretion probably capped by rising fuel subsidies; non-oil revenue growth to match inflation

Monetary tightening needs to be sustained to tame inflation, stabilize FX and force more fiscal consolidation

A preference to avoid markets as a funding source for large government fiscal deficits in recent years has subjugated monetary policy. As a consequence, base money has nearly doubled in real terms since 2015, surging to just over 10% of GDP in 2023. Debt monetization and unfunded CA deficits have pushed headline inflation from single digits in 2015 to 31.7%oya as at February 2024. Economic growth has remained below potential as numerous restrictions were introduced to limit the loss of FX reserves. Under new leadership, the CBN has embarked on a tightening path with a 400bp hike to the policy rate in February amid other liquidity tightening measures (see here). We see this as a step in the right direction and a strong show of intent, but the fiscal policy stance yet remains loose.

In our view, the central bank (CBN) should sustain monetary tightening, while authorities should also reduce fiscal dominance to stand a strong chance of sustainably curbing inflation and currency pressures. Fiscal tightening in coordination with monetary tightening would be needed to achieve the optimal policy mix. In an earlier piece, we explored Nigeria<sup>’</sup>s monetary policy framework and how the government<sup>’</sup>s budgetary position has constrained monetary policy. This note, however, focuses on Nigeria<sup>’</sup>s fiscal policy direction and the constraints it faces to effectively achieve a firm fiscal consolidation path at this time.

## Fiscal dominance hurdle

As the Federal Government of Nigeria<sup>’</sup>s (FGN) revenues started to come under pressure between 2010-2014, expenditures were curtailed to keep the overall fiscal deficit in check and below the fiscal responsibility rule of 3% of GDP. However, since the oil price collapse in 2015, the previous administration embarked on broadly countercyclical fiscal policies to support growth. Total expenditure as a share of GDP rose steadily from 5.3% in 2014 to 9.6% in 2023 despite revenues lingering at just over 4% of GDP (Figure 1). As a result, the overall FGN fiscal deficit rose to 5.5% of GDP in 2023 from just 1% recorded in 2014. Despite loose fiscal policy, growth was below potential, averaging just 1.5% over the period amid capital flow restrictions, less orthodox FX policies and oil production declines.

Figure 1: FGN's fiscal developments
![](images/51c21b9a5418277873623c15c8ad8d991e9a78557f1d2904c53c12af0fc21171.jpg)
Source: Budget office, CBN, J.P. Morgan

The fiscal expansion since 2015 was primarily financed by debt (Figure 2), including via direct fiscal financing from the central bank<sup>’</sup>s ways and means account. Hence the government<sup>’</sup>s liability to the central bank rose from 4% of total domestic debt in 2014 to 44% of total domestic debt at end 2023 . A key concern is that that monetary funding of the deficit in an environment of persistent USD demand is incompatible with FX and inflation stabilization.

However, a policy shift appear to be underway under new leadership. The recent 400bp hike to the policy rate and the hike of the cash reserve ratio to 45% (from 32.5%) are, in our view, steps in the right direction. But more tightening will be required to achieve the right overall policy stance. As part of that policy mix, fiscal policy also needs to be tighter to facilitate the phasing out of fiscal financing. However, as authorities are constrained by rising social pressures and higher debt service bills, substantial fiscal consolidation is challenging and likely requires further reduction also in the fuel subsidy.

Figure 2: FGN's fiscal deficit and funding sources
![](images/5f63d8fcc900b8db4e05d7d16e5106be08eb9bfe1ca2b90c7e7c42490da4e709.jpg)

## Revenue growth to face further headwinds

After an attempt at removing the fuel subsidy in June 2023, the authorities have now capped domestic fuel prices amid rising social pressures, which impacts government revenues. As the country still largely imports refined fuel, the significant depreciation of the currency has impacted the landing costs of petrol. We now estimate uncapped retail prices should be close to NGN1085.9 (US\$0.7), while the capped price now is NGN668/ltr (US\$0.4) (Table1). At these levels, we think fuel subsidy costs could rise to as much as NGN7.6tr (2.7% of GDP) this year from NGN1.8tr (0.8% of GDP) last year, and above the previous high of NGN4.4tr (2.2% of GDP) recorded in 2021. The subsidy essentially lowers government revenue as it affects the oil revenue accretion paid by the Nigerian National Petroleum Company Limited (NNPC Ltd) to the FGN, which is net of subsidies. Even so, we expect FGN oil revenues to rise somewhat to NGN3.4tr (1.2% of GDP) in 2024 from an estimated NGN1.7trn (0.8% of GDP) in 2023 due to the weaker currency and higher oil production (Figure 3).

Source: PPPRA, J.P. Morgan.

Figure 3: Oil revenues and subsidies
![](images/857e946f7a3cb986f1f786413df1f47f4eeaf89b455d4fd09a7f80ba0948ceec.jpg)
Source: Haver analytics, J.P. Morgan

Non-oil revenues have outperformed in recent years due to an increase in the VAT rate (from 5% to 7.5%) and improved tax collection but the path going forward is unclear. A revenue reform committee has been set up by the president to tackle issues around tax evasion, multiple taxation and overall tax administration, yet tax policy changes are not under consideration in the near term. We only anticipate a marginal rise in the non-oil tax to GDP ratio this year.

## Consolidated fiscal deficit only a tad lower

We expect the FGN primary deficit to narrow to 1% of GDP this year from 2.7% in 2023 on lower capex execution and higher revenues. However, higher interest costs will keep the fiscal deficit elevated at 5% this year (FGN: 3.9%), from 5.5% in 2023. A structural reduction in spending is difficult to achieve amidst a rising debt service bill and upward drift in the wage bill. The FGN<sup>’</sup>s debt service costs are expected to surge this year on the back of higher interest rates amid tighter monetary policy. Also, a hike in public sector and minimum wages is likely also in the cards amid ongoing social pressures. We expect wages and debt service to account for 70% of total FGN expenditure in 2024 (Table 2) while the debt service to revenue ratio picks back up to 89% this year from 66% in 2023. We think external financing of the budget deficit could rise to 2.3% of GDP this year with Eurobond issuances and World Bank budget funding in the cards. We expect ways and means financing to only amount to 0.4% of GDP as the CBN claims to have halted that line of financing.

deficit</td><td>-0.6</td><td>-0.6</td><td>-0.7</td><td>-0.6</td><td>-0.9</td><td>-1.3</td></tr><tr><td>Consolidated deficit</td><td>-2.3</td><td>-3.5</td><td>-4.5</td><td>-4.4</td><td>-6.4</td><td>-6.3</td></tr><tr><td>FGN deficit financing</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Net domestic</td><td>0.8</td><td>2.0</td><td>3.6</td><td>3.8</td><td>5.7</td><td>3.1</td></tr><tr><td>o/w ways and means</td><td>0.3</td><td>1.3</td><td>2.6</td><td>2.5</td><td>2.7</td><td>0.4</td></tr><tr><td>Net foreign</td><td>0.9</td><td>0.9</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.3</td><td>-0.2</td><td>2.3</td></tr></table>

Source: Budget office, J.P. Morgan

As Nigeria is a federation, it is also relevant for the overall fiscal picture to assess the consolidated fiscal deficit which includes that of the 36 states and the Federal Capital Territory (FCT). The state governments are less viable; as such, they depend heavily on statutory allocations from the Federal government (FAAC), which typically account for c.50% of their revenues. FAAC allocations to the states have increased in recent months due to exchange rate gains following the currency depreciation (Figure 4). Still, state government revenue likely will remain flat at 3% of GDP this year but higher

expenditures mostly on the back of likely minimum wage increases will push their deficits wider to 1.3% of GDP from 0.9% recorded in 2023. This level of fiscal deficit is probably masked by significant arrears from some the states. While the quantum of these arrears are unknown, it has been widely reported that some of the states owe workers salaries ranging from one month to three years.

Figure 4: Nigeria monthly FAAC distribution
![](images/b2a17b09e5001c6002c307d62e92f92e9ede7e25886728aa2605f0abf0fda818.jpg)
Source: National Bureau of Statistics, J.P. Morgan

On a consolidated basis, i.e., including the states, we estimate a fiscal deficit of 6.3% of GDP in 2024, marginally lower than 6.4% recorded in 2023 albeit nominally higher at NGN17.7tr from NGN14.7tr (Figure 5). The risk is to the upside, given the accelerated pace of borrowing already this year, where gross domestic issuance (Treasury bills and bonds) of NGN9.3tr YTD is already above the NGN6.0trn prescribed for the full year in the 2024 budget. This is likely associated with some repayment of the outstanding NGN7.3tr ways and means balance to the CBN. Although the Nigerian Senate has approved the securitization of the said balance, the CBN governor has insisted the balance as at Dec 2023 must be repaid. Presumably, authorities are looking to transfer some of the CBN debt to market debt, which would put further pressure on near-term domestic interest expense amid higher rates.

Figure 5: Nigeria's consolidated fiscal deficit
![](images/2068afb1989a3c229fe8d6a8e438b73a41edb7e260422f084aba8fda9613dede.jpg)
Source: Budget Office, Haver Analytics, J.P. Morgan

To sustainably ease the currency pressures and lower inflation, we think the CBN should sustain both price and liquidity tightening measures over the coming months, along with a helping hand from fiscal authorities by way of a revenue-led fiscal consolidation. We estimate Nigeria should be running primary surpluses between 2-3% of GDP to ease fiscal pressures. Authorities have an ambitious target of doubling the consolidated revenue-to-GDP ratio within the next five years. We think such a lofty goal is not feasible in an environment of sub-optimal growth, no tax policy changes and a weak tax base.

Furthermore, persistent social pressures amid high inflation will likely keep government spending elevated. As such, we expect overall fiscal deficit levels will remain high above 5% of GDP over the next two years with continued primary deficits. This likely allows fiscal dominance to be sustained a little while longer albeit reduced. For the CBN, we expect an additional 200bp hike at the upcoming MPC meeting, taking the policy rate to 24.75%.

## United States

• Median SEP dot consistent with three 25bp rate cuts this year starting in June

• Housing data firmed this week

• March business surveys looked mixed

• Next week: expect core PCE inflation at 2.8%oya and 0.2% monthly gain for real consumption

This week<sup>’</sup>s FOMC meeting didn<sup>’</sup>t produce large surprises, but the most notable outcome of that meeting was several remarks from Fed chair Powell that left little doubt about his intent to start cutting rates in the not-too-distant future. He was decidedly not worried about the January and February inflation prints, regarding them instead as “bumps in the road<sup>”</sup> in the ongoing journey to disinflation. Moreover, he did not see easing financial conditions as a risk, stating rather that such conditions “are weighing on economic activity<sup>”</sup> as evinced by the cooling in labor demand. After his remarks we remain comfortable with our outlook for a first rate cut in June followed by a quarterly tempo of rate cuts thereafter.

Even with Chair Powell<sup>’</sup>s clearly dovish remarks this week, Fed policy is not on autopilot and will depend on the data. Should inflation prove much peskier than Powell<sup>’</sup>s optimistic remarks there is a risk that the first rate cut is delayed past June. However, the risks are two-sided. One of the very few changes to Powell<sup>’</sup>s prepared remarks was to add the phrase “an unexpected weakening in the labor market could also warrant a policy response.<sup>”</sup> If such a weakening would occur then we believe the pace of rate cuts would be sped up to every meeting or, if the weakening were sharp enough, the size of the rate cuts would be increased.

The data flow this week delivered some upside surprises on housing. Existing home sales last month surprised to the upside, though related data on pending home sales suggest this may be a one-off. Housing starts and building permits also came in stronger than expected in February. In a note this week (US: The economic effects of surging immigration) we discuss how higher immigration may be contributing to the need for more housing units, and that this is a factor behind the surprisingly tight housing market. In this week<sup>’</sup>s Focus (More housing units to be completed soon) we discuss how the housing supply pipeline may soon ease some of that tightness. Immigration also has been an important factor in the labor market that has allowed for strong job growth without a decline in the unemployment rate. We think strong inflows of immigration could continue, and we correspondingly raised our estimates of nonfarm employment growth for the remainder of the year without lowering our forecast for the unemployment rate.

Other reports from the past week continued the mixed tone of the March business surveys. And the jobless claims data continued to signal that the labor market remains strong. There are many more reports scheduled for release next week, including February prints on consumer spending, new and pending existing home sales, durable goods orders and shipments, international trade, and some types of business inventories. Overall we think the data will be consistent with economic growth moderating in 1Q and we continue to forecast 2.25% real GDP growth for that quarter. Inflation data also will remain in focus, and we estimate that the core PCE price index rose 0.32% in February, with this measure up 2.8%oya. This year-ago reading is consistent with the estimate provided by Powell in the latest FOMC press conference, although our forecast for the monthly change is apparently higher than that of the Fed.

## FOMC looking to June

At the March FOMC meeting, the median FOMC participant was still looking for three rate cuts this year, though it now sees a shallower path of cutting in <sup>’</sup>25 and <sup>’</sup>26 compared to the last forecast in December. But Chair Powell had the final word and his overall message was dovish. The only notable change to his prepared remarks was to flag potential weakness in the labor market as a cause to cut rates. And in the Q&A he took a relaxed attitude to the January-February inflation pop and said that despite those readings the inflation “story is really essentially the same.<sup>”</sup> So it<sup>’</sup>s starting to look like, barring some adverse inflation developments, the plan is to start a quarterly tempo of rate cuts beginning in June, consistent with our forecast. Powell also said it would be appropriate to slow the pace of QT “fairly soon,<sup>”</sup> which we heard as the next meeting when we now expect them to reduce the monthly caps in half.

Returning to the main event, the FOMC unanimously chose to leave the fed funds target unchanged yet again. The postmeeting statement was very little changed from the one released after the January FOMC meeting: the only change was to drop the January statement<sup>’</sup>s reference to job gains having moderated since early last year. Turning to the median economic forecasts and the median dots, the <sup>’</sup>24 forecast was revised hawkishly but the median dot unchanged, whereas the <sup>’</sup>25 and <sup>’</sup>26 forecasts were little changed, but the dots revised hawkishly. The much-anticipated upward revision to the median longer-run dot finally happened, but in a very anticlimactic manner: up from 2.5% to 2.563%.

In Powell<sup>’</sup>s press conference, the prepared remarks changed very little from the January meeting, and most of those changes were just descriptive of the new forecasts. So it was notable that they added the phrase “and an unexpected weakening in the labor market could also warrant a policy

response,<sup>”</sup> though at various points in the press conference Powell said they have no reason to believe that such a weakening was likely. Where the press conference turned quite dovish was his description of the January-February upside inflation surprises. On January he thought it was reasonable to think there was some residual seasonality boosting that number. And on the February reading, while it was also high he said they were getting a PCE reading “well below 30 basis points.<sup>”</sup> Stepping back, he also said they weren<sup>’</sup>t taking the soft readings in the last seven months of last year as the new trend, and aren<sup>’</sup>t taking the firm readings of the last two months as a new trend. Rather, these are the “bumps in the road<sup>”</sup> that are inevitable in the disinflation process. Added to this optimism, he maintained his confidence that it<sup>’</sup>s just a matter of time before the market rents show up in the official rent data. Asked about financial conditions, Powell asserted they are weighing on economic activity and pointed to slowing labor demand as evidence of that.

Finally, on the balance sheet, in his prepared remarks Chair Powell indicated it would “be appropriate to slow the pace of balance sheet runoff fairly soon.<sup>”</sup> We believe this means they could decide to cut the monthly Treasury cap in half as soon as the May meeting, with implementation starting in mid-May. Powell also indicated that right now they<sup>’</sup>re mostly discussing slowing the pace of QT, and that issues like the composition and duration of the balance sheet are less pressing and can be debated later. We expect to learn more about their QT plans in the minutes to the March meeting released on April 10.

## Strong housing for now

This week brought upside surprises in the latest data on existing home sales, housing starts and permits, and homebuilder sentiment and all of these measures have shown improvement on net in recent months (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Select housing indicators
![](images/09ea362627af80b67590e240948186dacfa5476f2acdc1e3aac62c8a1a704aca.jpg)

Activity in the housing market likely has responded favorably to the decline in mortgage rates late last year, and we think that real residential investment is on track to jump in 1Q , with growth of around 11% saar expected for the quarter. But there are also reasons to think that this upward momentum won<sup>’</sup>t be sustained. For one, mortgage rates have increased somewhat since the start of the year. Furthermore, the recent pop in existing home sales looks stronger than the related pending home sales data, which are often a good leading indicator for existing home sales.

## March surveys mixed

The flash PMI reports showed mixed changes in momentum in March relative to February. The headline composite on the manufacturing survey increased from 52.2 to 52.5 and the headline activity index for the services survey cooled from 52.3 to 51.7 (Figure 2). The latest reading on the manufacturing survey is not particularly robust, but there has been solid improvement—the headline jumped 4.6pts over the past three months. The important output index has behaved similarly within the manufacturing survey, including a move up from 53.5 to 54.9 reported most recently between February and March. Meanwhile, the headline for the services PMI has been drifting roughly sideways, with small declines in February and March following modest increases over the prior few months. The survey<sup>’</sup>s indexes on new business and employment also haven<sup>’</sup>t changed very much on net in recent months.

Figure 2: PMI survey measures of output/activity
![](images/6ba90546b204ef80861aa05c81abb202c9907e4c56374614ac66821caec7252a.jpg)

Similarly, there have been mixed results across other business surveys reported for March so far. The link between the surveys and the “hard<sup>”</sup> activity data is not particularly strong, so perhaps the clearest message to take from the recent survey data is that they are not pointing to a big shift in economic activity one way or the other. Initial claims filings also have been pretty steady, which suggests that conditions in the labor market have not changed very much lately. The persistent low level of jobless claims filings is indicative of continued labor market strength.

For the shipments data, we believe increases associated with transportation helped push overall shipments up 0.4% in February. But we look for some pullback in core capital goods shipments following a solid January increase, with shipments down 0.3% in February.

We estimate that 4Q real GDP growth will be revised up from 3.2% to 3.4% between the BEA<sup>’</sup>s second and third reports on growth for the quarter. Since the recent GDP report on February 28, we think the updated construction spending data and updated international trade data point to solid upward revisions in some related GDP categories. In related categories, we forecast that real growth in nonresidential structures will be revised up from 7.5% to 12.3% saar and that real growth in government spending will be revised up from 4.2% to 4.5% saar along with an upward revision in the 4Q real trade balance from -\$915bn to -\$907bn saar. Partially offsetting these upward revisions, we expect to see downward revisions to real growth in consumer spending (from 3.0% to 2.8% saar) and residential investment (from 2.9% to 2.5% saar) along with the real change in business inventories (from \$66bn to \$63bn saar).

The upcoming GDP report also will contain the BEA<sup>’</sup>s first official estimate of corporate profits for 4Q and we think solid growth in economic activity will translate into solid growth in profits during the quarter. We estimate that nominal profits rose 1.8% saqr in 4Q.

## Fri Personal income

<table><tr><td colspan="2">Mar 29 %m/m, sa, unless noted 8:30am</td><td colspan="4"></td></tr><tr><td></td><td></td><td>Nov</td><td>Dec</td><td>Jan</td><td>Feb</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Personal income</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.3</td><td>1.0</td><td>0.6</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Wages &amp; salaries</td><td>0.4</td><td>0.5</td><td>0.4</td><td>0.6</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Consumption</td><td>0.4</td><td>0.7</td><td>0.2</td><td>0.6</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Real consumption</td><td>0.4</td><td>0.6</td><td>-0.1</td><td>0.2</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>PCE price index</td><td>0.0</td><td>0.1</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.4</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Core</td><td>0.09</td><td>0.14</td><td>0.42</td><td>0.32</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Mkt-Based Core</td><td>0.1</td><td>0.1</td><td>0.3</td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Core (%oya)</td><td>3.2</td><td>2.9</td><td>2.8</td><td>2.8</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Mkt-Based Core (%oya)</td><td>3.2</td><td>3.0</td><td>2.8</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Saving rate</td><td></td><td>4.1</td><td>3.7</td><td>3.8</td><td>3.6</td></tr></table>

Inflation data remain in focus, and with the February CPI and PPI data already in hand, we believe the PCE price index rose 0.4% in February while the core price deflator rose 0.32%. With the solid monthly gain expected for the headline measure, we think that year-ago inflation for the PCE price index will climb from 2.4% to 2.5%oya between January and February. While it does appear that core PCE inflation has picked up over the past few months, we expect continued moderation for the related year-ago rate, although with rounding the core inflation rate likely will stay unchanged at 2.8% between January and February. For the PCE price index for core services excluding housing (the “supercore<sup>”</sup>), we forecast a 0.3% monthly increase for February with this measure up 3.4% oya (vs. 3.5% in January).

We see mixed signals in figures related to consumer spending in February including a surge in unit auto sales during the month but a plunge in utilities production likely related to a drop-off in demand associated with changes in the weather. On the whole, it looks like February was a so-so month for real consumption growth, with this aggregate up 0.2% during the month. With prices up in February, we look for stronger growth in nominal spending, with consumption up 0.6%.

We forecast that nominal income increased 0.6% in February, supported by a solid 0.6% increase in wages and salaries. The employment report pointed to strong wage growth in February, helped by an increase in the workweek. While we think that income growth will be strong in February, we do expect some cooling relative to the January increase, which was supported by a jump in transfer payments that likely won<sup>’</sup>t be repeated in February. Disposable income growth is projected to be softer than total income growth in February, with an increase in taxes anticipated for the month. And with our expectations for disposable income and consumer spending, we believe the saving rate declined 0.2%-pt in February.

We estimate that the nominal trade balance widened from -\$90.5bn in January to -\$91.5bn in February, with exports rising 1.4% and imports up 1.3%. The trade deficit has been widening in recent months, in part because of strong growth in imports. Port data point to another increase in imports in February, which we think will help widen the deficit again. We do also look for some firming in exports growth, however, to offset some of the February increase in imports.

<table><tr><td rowspan="3">Fri Mar 29 8:30am Autos</td><td rowspan="3">Business inventories (adv.) %m/m, sa, unless noted</td><td colspan="3"></td></tr><tr><td>Nov</td><td>Dec</td><td>Jan</td></tr><tr><td>-0.4</td><td>0.4</td><td>-0.3</td><td>Feb 0.2</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="3">Wholesale Retail inventories</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>0.0 -0.8</td><td>0.6 0.4</td><td>0.7</td></tr><tr><td>Ex autos</td><td>0.4 2.0 1.1</td><td>0.3 0.8</td><td>0.3 1.6</td></tr></table>

We believe nominal wholesale inventories increased 0.2% in February while nominal retail inventories jumped 0.7%. Auto industry figures point to another jump in related retail inventories, but we also look for growth in other types of inventories on net in February, in part because energy price increases are expected to boost the nominal values relative to January.

## Homebuilders survey (Mar 18)

<table><tr><td></td><td>Jan</td><td>Feb</td><td>Mar</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Housing market</td><td>44</td><td>48</td><td>48</td><td>51</td></tr><tr><td>Present sales</td><td>48</td><td>52</td><td></td><td>56</td></tr><tr><td>Prospective buyer traffic</td><td>29</td><td>33 32</td><td></td><td>34</td></tr></table>

The NAHB survey<sup>’</sup>s headline beat expectations in March, rising 3-pts to 51 that month. While there have been some ups and downs across a variety of different housing indicators lately, the NAHB<sup>’</sup>s gauge of homebuilder sentiment clearly has been moving higher, with increases in each of the past four reported months (for a cumulative 17-pt jump). This improvement in sentiment started around the time when mortgage rates started moving down last fall and it has continued through the mixed changes in rates reported so far this year. While the increase in homebuilder sentiment has been significant lately, it came off of a pretty low level for homebuilder attitudes.

<table><tr><td rowspan="2">Mililion units, saar</td><td colspan="2">Dec</td><td colspan="2">Jan</td><td colspan="2">Feb</td></tr><tr><td>1.56</td><td>1.57</td><td>1.33</td><td>1.37</td><td>137</td><td>1.52</td></tr><tr><td>Starts Single-family starts</td><td>1.05</td><td>1.06</td><td>1.00</td><td>1.01</td><td>1.03</td><td>1.13</td></tr><tr><td>Multifamily starts</td><td>0.51</td><td>0.50</td><td>0.33</td><td>0.36</td><td>0.34</td><td>0.39</td></tr><tr><td>Permits</td><td>1.49</td><td></td><td>1.49</td><td></td><td>1-50</td><td>1.52</td></tr></table>

The February starts and permits data beat expectations, with housing starts jumping 10.7% to 1.521mn saar that month and related permits rising 1.9% to 1.518mn saar. There were gains reported across the main details of the report, with February increases in activity reported for the single-family data (starts: 11.6%, permits: 1.0%) and the multifamily figures (starts: 8.3%, permits: 4.1%). While the jump in starts reported for February was large, it did come after a drop in activity reported for January. Zooming out a bit more from the month-tomonth changes, the data on single-family activity generally have been trending higher lately while the data on multifamily activity have come down over the past 12 months even with the February gains. While we have seen some cooling in the multifamily starts and permits data lately, there is still a large number of related units currently under construction, and we think limits in available inventory are continuing to generate a need for construction activity.

## S&P Global services PMI (US flash)(Mar 21)

<table><tr><td></td><td>Jan</td><td>Feb</td><td>Mar</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Business activity</td><td>52.5</td><td>52.3</td><td>52.0</td><td>51.7</td></tr><tr><td>Incoming new business</td><td>52.6</td><td>51.9</td><td></td><td>51.8</td></tr><tr><td>Employment</td><td>51.5</td><td>51.2</td><td></td><td>51.2</td></tr><tr><td>Business expectations</td><td>68.7</td><td>65.5</td><td></td><td>70.3</td></tr><tr><td>Input prices</td><td>56.8</td><td>55.6</td><td></td><td>59.3</td></tr><tr><td>Prices charged</td><td>51.8</td><td>53.9</td><td></td><td>56.6</td></tr><tr><td>Backlogs of work</td><td>51.9</td><td>48.6</td><td></td><td>48.3</td></tr></table>

In the flash PMI reports for the month, the headline composite on the manufacturing survey increased from 52.2 to 52.5 and the headline activity index for the services survey cooled from 52.3 to 51.7. The latest reading on the manufacturing survey is not particularly robust, but there has been solid improvement over the past few months—the headline jumped 4.6pts over the past three months. The important output index has behaved similarly within the manufacturing survey, including a move up from 53.5 to 54.9 reported most recently between February and March. Despite the recent improvement in these surveys, the separate Empire State manufacturing survey has been much more downbeat (through March), and the “hard<sup>”</sup> data on manufacturing output from the Fed<sup>’</sup>s IP report have been soft (through February).

Shifting back away from the manufacturing sector, the headline for the services PMI has been drifting roughly sideways, with small declines in February and March following modest increases over the prior few months. The survey<sup>’</sup>s indexes on new business and employment similarly haven<sup>’</sup>t changed very much on net in recent months.

## Existing home sales (Mar 21)

<table><tr><td></td><td>Dec</td><td>Jan</td><td>Feb</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Total (mn, saar)</td><td>3.88</td><td>4.00</td><td>3.95</td><td>4.38</td></tr><tr><td>%m/m</td><td>-0.8</td><td>3.1</td><td>1.2</td><td>9.5</td></tr><tr><td>%oya nsa</td><td>-9.2</td><td>1.3</td><td>12.8</td><td>0.7</td></tr><tr><td>Months&#x27; supply (nsa)</td><td>3.1</td><td>3.0</td><td></td><td>2.9</td></tr><tr><td>Single-family</td><td>3.0</td><td>3.0</td><td></td><td>2.8</td></tr><tr><td>Median price (%oya)</td><td>4.1</td><td>5.1</td><td>4.9</td><td>5.7</td></tr></table>

Existing home sales jumped 9.5% to 4.38mn saar in February. This large increase built on the 3.1% move up reported for January and was significantly above expectations. Several housing-related indicators have firmed in recent months, and it is likely the recent declines in rates have helped support homebuying activity. But the latest existing home sales figure looks stronger than the recent related data on pending home sales, so we likely will see some cooling in existing home sales to come after the February upside surprise.

## Focus: More housing units to be completed soon

There were 1.521mn saar housing starts in February. This was an upside surprise relative to expectations but since mid-2022 the starts data generally have been running below the figures reported over the prior year and a half or so (Figure 1). This relative cooling in starts likely has led to a bit of a move down in the number of housing units currently under construction, although at 1.681mn in February, the number of units under construction remained elevated by pre-pandemic norms and broader historical standards (Figure 2). We think many of these houses that are currently under construction are due to be completed fairly soon, and we could see completions over the next six months average an annualized rate of about 1.5mn per month, a solid rate that should help alleviate some of the current shortages of housing supply.

Figure 1: Housing starts
![](images/7867c6e0e1305e8d9e6b9c89d5812b77077eda1acb9a2af6d3b689476145fb7b.jpg)

Figure 2: Housing units under construction
![](images/0754584423a282738b303b5c16038564b9186d4a0fa3c344ba682638ce3db316.jpg)

In recent years, single-family units have been taking around eight to nine months on average from start to completion, a bit longer than the norm from earlier decades. Multifamily units, meanwhile, take much longer, with about a 17-month lag time from start to completion in recent years, which is also up from historical norms (Figure 3). By applying these lag times to the recent starts data, we can predict when the units under construction will be completed (Figures 4 and 5<sup>1</sup>).

Figure 3: Average length of time from start to completion Months
![](images/e95439bc2a1ad85cbdf15b6109b29e30d2aa64fa31e26e9fe08c76d5e5ee5856.jpg)

Single-family completions jumped up in February, with almost 1.1mn units finished that month (annualized). This was higher than the rough implication from the related starts data, but the starts data suggest more completions to come over time, with perhaps more than 1mn per month later this year (Figure 4). The near-660,000 completed multifamily units from February (annualized) also was above the general implication from the related starts data, but the starts figures suggest a high level of completions for upcoming months before some tapering off late in the year (Figure 5).

Figure 4: Completions of single-family housing units
![](images/f4da7c9f74be8a9c470a952c7b97526ad9152b19108b8ac47ea026dbcb6c1480.jpg)

Figure 5: Completions of multifamily housing units
![](images/cda8ab19258e20ed3b064f351a44560e87826f9e3f2a33fa806160cdf4827a79.jpg)

## Euro Area

• PMI rises further in March; German manufacturing still weak

• Consumer confidence rebounds; we expect it to increase further

• Core inflation firm overall in the early part of the year

Euro area activity data point to firmer growth. The further rise in the PMI leaves the survey consistent with our 0.5% ar 1Q growth forecast. The March level also is already aligned with our 0.75% ar GDP forecast, thus some further increase in the survey would create upside risks around our 2Q GDP forecast. The details of the survey were encouraging but the German manufacturing sector remains the weak spot. The 0.5pt increase in the manufacturing output index still leaves the survey at a very low level (42.8). On the inflation front, the February details did not show any major distortion. Looking through the noise, we thus think that the underlying pace of core inflation was close to 3.5% ar in the early part of the year, up from the 3% ar pace seen in 4Q. We continue to expect the core prices gains to softened in the coming months and take comfort from recent business survey (EC and PMI). This would bring core inflation to 2.8%oya in 2Q and 2.7%oya in 2H24. However, we acknowledge some upside risks due to the strong January-February HICP releases.

## PMI now signals 0.75% ar growth

The flash March Euro area PMI increased 0.7pt to 49.9, a stronger increase than we and consensus expected (49.5 and 49.7, respectively). The 1Q survey data on average are consistent with our view that GDP will expand by 0.5%q/q ar, a slight step up compared the broad stagnation of the economy in 2H23. The trend up in the data is also aligned with our forecast for GDP growth to firm in 2Q. The PMI survey only would need to stabilize at the March level to be consistent with our 0.75% ar forecast (Figure 1). We are not making any change to our GDP forecast but a further PMI increase would create upside risks.

Figure 1: Euro area GDP
![](images/a3031fa268243e5e91aeb2494d1da1ad3e389ee6772b6d15d6e12a3ef9dbe01e.jpg)

The survey composite details overall were encouraging. The employment index declined 0.7pt to 50.5, but the level still points to private employment growth at 0.8% ar. The demand side is also improving with a further gain in the new orders index (+0.6 to 49.2), and the future output index (a measure of confidence) continued to trend up (+0.2pt to 60.7). Input and output price indices meanwhile declined (-1.3pts to 57.1 and -1.3pts to 53.1, respectively), while the data had been on an upward trend since the summer. Prince indices overall are close to levels seen over 2017-2019 when prices pressures were contained.

The report nonetheless continues to hint at a weak manufacturing sector. The Euro area manufacturing output index was up 0.2pt to 46.8 with Germany being particularly weak, a 0.5 gain in this country to 42.8 only being a small offset to the 3.4pts decline seen in February. The PMI press release suggested that this weakness is not linked to shipping issues on the Red Sea. In fact, delivery times have shortened according the Euro area data. The services sector thus remains the main driver of the recovery. The services output index remains on an upward trend (+0.9 to 51.1 in March) with demand and confidence indicators still rising.

The country data overall feel more balanced and were consistent with our view that the March Euro area increase would be driven to a significant extent by Germany. The German composite PMI jumped 1.2pts to 47.4 (a move skewed towards services), largely offsetting declines seen in the prior three months (-1.5pts cumulatively). Meanwhile, the composite survey declined 0.4pt to 47.7 in France. But this comes after a huge 3.5pts gain the prior month. Thus, some payback was likely, and the March reading is in our view consolidating the huge February gain. The country details finally continue to show that the rest of the region (i.e. Italy and Spain, to a large extent) are leading the recovery. The composite PMI index increased 1.1pts to 53.6 outside Germany and France (details for Italy and Spain will be published in early April).

## Consumer confidence nudges up

Consumer confidence is key as we expect household spending to gain momentum on the back of strong real income gains. In this week<sup>’</sup>s flash report, the European Commission consumer confidence survey increased 0.6pt to 15.5%bal in February (Figure 2). This increase does not offset the 1.0%pt decline in January, but looking through the noise, the survey overall has been on an upward trend since the autumn. No details were released this week.

Figure 2: Euro area consumer confidence Standard deviations from 1999-2019 avg
![](images/601ef045fb9e5909001f18fb7ed7e099e9d31142ec54fc7ec77dff037a8191e3.jpg)

Despite recent improvements, the survey remains fairly low by historical standards. This outcome partly reflects the fact that households<sup>’</sup> views about the labor market do not feed into the consumer confidence aggregate. Labor markets have been tight, and households overall have been optimistic about recent labor market dynamics (this can be seen in a separate question). The alternative ECB consumer survey also shows that households are seeing the decline in inflation, but less than the actual decline in inflation. We expect this gap to close over time, which should boost consumer confidence further in the coming months.

## Core prices firm through the noise

The February Euro area HICP data were left broadly unchanged in this week<sup>’</sup>s release. According to Eurostat, headline inflation declined 0.2%-pt to 2.6%oya, with core inflation down 0.2%-pt to 3.1%oya. The ECB seasonally adjusted data continue to show a 0.41%m/m sa gain in headline prices in February, with core prices now up 0.37%m/m (versus a 0.35%m/m gain at the time of the flash release).

The full February details were released for the first time this week and are important to assess. Taxes had no significant impact on core prices according to Eurostat, unlike January when VAT rates were reset higher across several items (including restaurant prices in Germany). Going through our seasonally adjusted detailed series, we notice some noise in the airfares category which points to a 0.1%-pt drag on core prices in January and a 0.1%-pt boost in February (thus cancelling each others over January-February).

Stripping out noise is hard but core prices in an underlying sense were up by about 0.3%m/m sa on average in the first two months of the year, in our view. Core prices thus have been running at a near-3.5% ar pace this year, while underlying inflationary pressures had come down to a 3% ar pace in 4Q23 (after controlling for basket weight changes last year).

The firmer core price pressure this year is both driven by core goods and services. Core goods prices have now been rising for three months, which contrasts with modest declines seen the prior three months. Core goods prices benefited from the unwinding of supply side issues and the gas price shock last year, which has now come to an end. However, shipping issues in the Red Sea so far do not seem to have had a significant impact on core goods prices (as was suggested by the PMI data).

Services prices meanwhile were up a firm 0.46%m/m sa on average over January-February. Some of this is driven by one-off insurance price and tax hikes (representing an average 0.1%-pt boost in our view), but the underlying pace is firmer than seen in 4Q (a bit less than 0.3%m/m sa). We argued in a recent piece that services prices are an accurate and timely measure of domestic inflation. Services prices thus suggest that domestic price pressures remain firm through February. The 4Q GDP details earlier this month showed that unit labor cost growth (5.8%oya in 4Q) and unit profit growth (2.8%oya) were overall firm at the end of last year, despite some declines in year-on-year readings.

We nonetheless take comfort from the latest EC price expectation data. These data in our view signal some easing in inflationary pressures driven by unit profits in 1H24. We thus continue to expect core price gains to move down to a 0.2%m/m sa pace in the coming months. This would bring core inflation to 2.8%oya in 2Q and 2.7%oya in 2H24 (Figure 3). We, however, acknowledge some upside risks due to the strong January-February HICP releases.

The ECB core forecast stands significantly lower than our forecast (2.5%oya on average over 2Q-4Q). Part of the gap (at least 0.1%-pt) is explained by the stronger-than-expected February print, which came after the ECB cut-off date .

According to the flash release, consumer confidence rebounded in March. The consumer data represent 20% of the total economic confidence indicator. On the business side, we take comfort from the latest PMI increase and penciled in a rise in the overall economic confidence index in March. The confidence details will be important to monitor. Confidence in the manufacturing sector likely will remain well below the services sector. The country details will also be important to monitor. The Italian and Spanish data in particular were not available as part of the flash PMI release. Thus the EC data will provide some valuable information for March beyond the flash PMI.

Demand and labor markets

<table><tr><td colspan="2">Retail sales</td><td rowspan="2">Dec</td><td rowspan="2">Jan</td><td rowspan="2">Feb</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Nov</td></tr><tr><td>Thu Mar 28</td><td>Germany</td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>8:00am</td><td>Sales ex. autos and petroleum, volumes, sa</td><td>-0.5 -0.4</td><td>-0.4</td><td>0.5</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>%m/m</td><td></td><td>-1.3</td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>%oya</td><td>-1.6</td><td>-0.5</td><td></td></tr></table>

German retail sales are notoriously volatile and prone to revision. This makes any first estimate hard to gauge. In a fundamental sense, we think that household real incomes are rising, reflecting increases in wages and lower inflation. As a result, we have pencilled in an increase for February, but with the caveat that first prints can be biased.

The German labor market has remains very resilient considering the prolonged weakness in real GDP. In fact, employment growth has accelerated again at the turn of the year. The business surveys suggest that employment growth has remained positive through this period. Unemployment has however

increased as the increases in employment have not fully kept up with the growth of the labor force. We expect these trends to have continued in March, leaving the labor market looking resilient overall.

Country-level March preliminary HICP releases will start to be published next week, ahead of the Euro area April 3 release. Euro area core prices in the first two months of the year have been sticky, with core prices running at an underlying 3.5% ar. We expect core prices to run at a below-3% ar pace in the coming months and the March flash estimate will be an important test. We take comfort from business surveys (EC and PMI) which recently have been consistent with easier price pressures in the early part of the year.

Euro area bank lending was weaker in January, but without altering the impression of some improvement in the trend. Nevertheless, given the decline, which was driven by loans to nonfinancial corporates, the February data will be important to see. The sense from the January Bank Lending Survey is that declines in loan demand are moderating and the extent of any credit tightening is abating. As a result, we suspect that the February M3 report will show some improvement from January.

## Japan

BoJ delivers at the hawkish end of expectations, but maintains dovish guidance

• A return to orthodox policy framework opens the door for further tightening...

• ...and we now look for two 25bp hikes this year; yen depreciation is a wildcard

• March surveys stabilize; Feb CPI remains firm

The BoJ removed all major unconventional policy measures this week, exiting NIRP, abolishing YCC, discontinuing ETF and J-REIT purchases, and returning to a single-tiering system for deposits held on its balance sheet. While the outcome sits at the hawkish end of expectations, the decision was wrapped in dovish guidance, with a commitment to keep financial conditions accommodative “for the time being<sup>”</sup> and maintain JGB purchases at “broadly the same amount.<sup>”</sup>

We think it would be a mistake, however, to conclude that the BoJ is done. Ueda emphasized that the BoJ would now return to an orthodox monetary policy framework, with the policy rate the key policy tool. If inflation continues to exceed the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s target, as we forecast, we expect the BoJ to respond through interest rate hikes. As such, we are bringing forward our call for monetary policy tightening, and now look for two 25bp rate hikes this year, in July and December, assuming the global economy does not slow sharply. We also think the BoJ is likely to reduce its balance sheet gradually. While the Bank is unlikely to carry out QT in a pre-scheduled manner, we expect a gradual reduction in its JGB purchases depending on market conditions.

The risk to our forecast of gradual policy adjustment hinges to some degree on the yen. If yen depreciation accelerates to the point that imported inflation significantly alters the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s own assumptions, we think the policy response could involve rate hikes. This would be a departure from prior episodes of yen weakness that have been met with currency intervention from the Ministry of Finance, but not with a monetary policy response. However, Ueda<sup>’</sup>s comments this week suggested sharp currency moves, if they lead to a large impact on the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s assessment of the outlook, could lead to a policy response. In this context, the BoJ will need to balance between reducing policy accommodation carefully in the face of still-sluggish domestic demand, but remain on the move so as to avoid sparking further household capital outflows that can exacerbate yen depreciation.

Incoming data give us confidence that underlying price momentum remains firm, likely prompting an upward reassessment of the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s own inflation forecasts. This week, nationwide core prices were up again in February, and services prices rose for an eighth consecutive month, with momentum accelerating to 2.3%3m/3m saar. March surveys, also released this week, suggest that services activity remains robust, and we expect a large rise in retail sales data next week. The weak link remains the manufacturing sector. While manufacturers reported some stabilization in activity going into March, the sector has been buffeted by earthquake-related supply-chain disruption, soft external demand, and auto sector plant shutdowns. We expect output to end the quarter in deep contraction.

## BoJ: Everything, all at once

At the press conference, Governor Ueda stated that the decision was backed by rising prospects for achieving the inflation target, but also admitted that the unconventional policy had lasted longer than initially expected. Combining this with the limited indication about additional hikes, we think the NIRP was seen as an extraordinary policy that needs to be abolished early. On the additional rate hikes, Governor Ueda maintained his cautious stance, stating that “we can proceed at a gradual pace<sup>”</sup>. There was no clear indication of additional hikes, but he stated that the BoJ likely will consider additional hikes if its outlook for inflation is rising or the risk of this is rising. We think this would mean that persistent yen depreciation could also trigger BoJ hikes.

We have long argued that the BoJ has a strong incentive to begin shrinking its balance sheet earlier than the market expects, and these decisions open the door to that. In fact, in the quarterly schedule of JGB purchases for the Apr-Jun period announced today, the BoJ reduced the maximum amount for all maturities excluding short-term bills, while keeping the minimum amount. Although this does not necessarily mean an immediate reduction from the current amount, the BoJ stated future purchases depend on “the market developments and supply and demand conditions of JGBs<sup>”</sup>, leaving it with considerable discretion. If inflation continues to exceed the target as we expect, the BoJ likely will need to begin preparations with an eye on raising policy rates to the neutral level (we estimate this to be around 1.5%) in the future, even if this is not an immediate plan. This naturally means a reduction in the balance sheet.　Governor Ueda confirmed this, stating that “the BoJ will reduce JGB purchases at one point in future.<sup>”</sup>　Although the BoJ is unlikely to carry out QT in a prescheduled manner, the Bank likely will continue to seek opportunities to reduce its purchases depending on market conditions.

Reflecting the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s rising confidence in achieving the target, we bring forward the timing of the additional hikes from our previous forecasts of end-24 and mid-25. The BoJ has deliberately delayed policy adjustments to support structural

changes in inflation dynamics. We expect the BoJ to try sticking to the “strategic<sup>”</sup> gradual policy normalization, as the economy is seen to be vulnerable to rapid rate hikes after the prolonged aggressive monetary easing. However, slow BoJ action could lead to persistent yen depreciation and/or an accumulation of financial system risks. If the global economy remains solid and other central banks<sup>’</sup> rate cuts remain limited, the BoJ will need to balance these two-sided risks. We now expect two rate hikes this year, in July and December, assuming the global economy does not slow significantly.

## March surveys stabilize

Japan<sup>’</sup>s March all-industry flash PMI rose 1.7 points to 52.3. The move up reflected a 1 point increase in the manufacturing PMI to 48.2 and a stronger 2 point rise in the services PMI, which at 54.9 is not far off its cycle high. The manufacturing output index also edged up, potentially reflecting some easing in auto-related supply-chain disruption that had weighed on output in early 1Q (Figures 1 and 2). Forward-looking components were mixed, with a rise in overall orders, but declines in export orders and future output indices (Figure 3).

Figure 1: Japan PMI mfg. output and manufacturing IP
![](images/f495b03bad6250f9293eba5eb4a8e8bd03bfa01e791b61f3c4b5145216303f9d.jpg)

Figure 2: Japan PMI services new business and BoJ CAI
![](images/eeb774d62afc4e7e106c9060c99b489e195741438c931144438f02793e5ef8c3.jpg)

The Reuters Tankan survey for March was also released this week, and sent a similar message as the PMI. Sentiment among large manufacturers picked up 11 points to 10, putting the index roughly where it was before a sharp decline through January and February. Non-manufacturing sentiment picked up to a stronger 32, reflecting continued rises in confidence among retailers.

Figure 3: Japan Manufacturing PMI - New orders
![](images/3a4287e2d10ce3f80868cc9648a1dc3c4ba515a0eb7f4ccf6cacaadba3dc0de8.jpg)

## Services prices still firm

The February nationwide CPI report remained firm, though on balance was a little softer than our forecasts. The global standard measure of core prices rose 0.2%m/m sa, a tenth below our expectations, leaving core inflation (ex. food and energy) at 2.5%oya. Other core measures were broadly as expected. By price category, core goods price momentum continues to ease, largely on the back of a deceleration in input costs, while services price momentum remains sticky (Figures 4 and 5).

Figure 4: Japan global core prices (ex. food and energy)
![](images/e328001d51ea5a16507fe87a14f679ad833eb490d35bde1cb64e30663833bbd9.jpg)

Figure 5: Japan nationwide services prices % chg.
![](images/ee46176d99ec3fec32da59f0877643a601eac88e7b177b75fb535773faa8b76e.jpg)

While a few technical factors are pushing up services prices, the underlying trend remains firm. Services prices were up 0.1%m/m sa, leaving services inflation at 2.2%oya. Services prices momentum accelerated to 2.3%3m/3m saar from 1.9% in January, the fastest pace of increase since October last year. And services prices have now risen on the month for eight consecutive months, the longest uninterrupted increase in at least 15 years. We think this trend will be a key focus for the BoJ, and the risk of upward revisions to the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s inflation forecasts should lead to the next increase in the policy rate, which we pencil in for July.

We also think the trajectory for the yen will be important in informing the inflation profile, and thus the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s policy reaction function. The passthrough from currency depreciation to consumer prices can be blunted by the impact of subsidies and lags in energy and food price setting behavior. But Ueda<sup>’</sup>s comments this week suggested sharp moves, if they lead to a large impact on the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s assessment of the outlook, could lead to a policy response.

## Canada

• Headline CPI decelerated in February to the most muted pace since March 2021

• The Bank of Canada<sup>’</sup>s core measures continued to ease

We see building confidence at the Bank of Canada that inflation is heading sustainably toward the 2% target

Headline CPI decelerated for a second month in February, down a tick to 2.8% year-over-year (2.78%oya to two decimals) compared with January and the most muted annual gain since March 2021. Importantly, measured on a three-month annualized basis the Bank of Canada<sup>’</sup>s core CPI measures and the underlying core CPI (excluding food and energy) are now unmistakably within the Bank<sup>’</sup>s 1% to 3% inflation targeting range (Figure 1). A second monthly downside surprise in 2024 and the sharp ease in underlying inflation should build some confidence at the Bank of Canada that inflation is heading sustainably toward the 2% target. A case could be made for the Bank to ease in April, but the its hawkish communication just two weeks ago has pushed back on that prospect. We maintain the Bank will cut the policy rate in June and expect its rhetoric to turn more dovish in the April monetary policy announcement. The Bank has already struck a more dovish tone in the minutes of the March meeting released this week. It was noted for the first time that the Governing Council had agreed conditions for rate cuts should materialize in 2024 if the economy evolves in line with the Bank<sup>’</sup>s projections.

Figure 1: Bank of Canada core CPIs
![](images/4f96936b648cfdee5f37121b0f230fdf20b519aadc62deb36ad928d06f72e7a2.jpg)
Source: Statistics Canada, J.P. Morgan

CPI annual growth in February was notably below our expectations. On a seasonally adjusted monthly basis, both the headline CPI and underlying core increased 0.1%. The Bank of Canada<sup>’</sup>s closely watched core measures decelerated for a second month, from 3.35%oya in January to 3.15%oya on average in February. Early in 2024, inflation is clearly evolving at a much slower pace than the BoC was expecting in its January outlook. With January and February inflation in the books, the 2.6%oya quarterly change in headline CPI inflation is tracking significantly below the Bank of Canada<sup>’</sup>s 3.2%oya forecast from the January MPR.

A significantly weaker contribution from durable goods prices (compared with January) and a substantial drag from weakness in semi-durable prices (like clothing and footwear) held down the pace of core goods prices annual growth. The annual pace of services prices excluding shelter services was about stable and still below the 2.0% target.

## Balance is a cycle not an achievement

This week Deputy Governor Toni Gravelle (who has become the pointperson on Bank of Canada balance sheet policy) discussed the evolution of quantitative tightening and when it might end. After two years of QT, the Bank<sup>’</sup>s asset holdings have declined by roughly 40% to \~C\$300 billion (Figure 2). And based on the profile of asset maturities, the Bank expects QT to end sometime in 2025, a bit later than previously estimated. Gravelle confirmed that balance sheet normalization would continue once the Bank has started cutting the policy rate as long as the policy rate remains in restrictive territory. The Bank of Canada has long held that the nominal neutral interest rate in Canada is estimated to be in the range of 2% to 3%.

Figure 2: Bank of Canada Balance Sheet
![](images/ca9fddca860c682e8b8715c49900e371554ec99243d639f146315132d058d946.jpg)

Once QT ends, the Bank plans to restore a more balanced mix of assets with a broader range of maturities similar to prepandemic; the sequence will likely be term repos first, then t-bills, with bond purchases coming later.The Bank is current ly weighing the pros and cons of reverting to the pandemic policy of buying GoC t-bills and bonds in the secondary market via reverse auctions or the pre-pandemic policy of purchasing assets in primary GoC debt auctions.The Bank foresees a multi-year transition period to a “steady-state<sup>”</sup> balance sheet as the stock of money market instruments—t-bills and term repo—are replenished. GoC bonds will remain the biggest asset type on the balance sheet, however. The Bank will no longer buy Canadian Mortgage Bonds. Note that the Bank of Canada is acting as agent for the Canadian government<sup>’</sup>s Canada Mortgage Bond (CMB) program.The government intends to purchase 50% of fixed-rate primary issuance of CMBs over the 2024 calendar year or an annual maximum of C\$30 billion.
