## Mexico

• As expected, Banxico cut 25bp to 11%, leaving the door open for further actions

• We now expect back-to-back cuts reaching 9.5% by year-end

• January GDP proxy confirms a bigger pothole

• We now eye 1Q GDP at -0.5% saar; full-year at 1.9%y/y

The March monetary policy decision delivered the broadly expected 25bp cut to 11%, with the most hawkish member dissenting in favor of an on-hold decision. Importantly, and contrary to our expectations, the statement emphasized that “future actions will be congruent with the required path to bring inflation to its target in the foreseeable future.<sup>”</sup> In our policy preview from last week, we expected the statement to emphasize that “this is not necessarily the beginning of an easing cycle...<sup>”</sup> a message that is usually identified with less conviction regarding future cuts.

While in both cases the board is adopting a careful message subject to the evolution of key gauges embedded in the reaction function, the emphasis on “future actions<sup>”</sup> is, in our view, an important message that communicates that the base-case scenario is for a steady easing cycle. The passive tightening that has occurred since the end of the hiking cycle last year is, in our view, the most important argument in favor of steady cuts ahead. As such, we now expect back-to-back cuts this year and next, bringing the policy rate to 9.5% in 2024 and to 7.5% in 2025.

We believe the case for a cycle is well described in the statement given the emphasis on the lingering restrictive stance; the mitigation of inflation shocks and the disinflation gains; a more comfortable stance with respect to core dynamics; and the fact that inflation expectations remain stable, close to 3.5%.

Figure 1:Quarterly inflation path: Headline inflation
![](images/e9db021d1dc4a3c12e25bad02fb0e7ac463ecf6934e3ee3110933a8cc6afd640.jpg)
Mar 21 Sep 21 Mar 22 Sep 22 Mar 23 Sep 23 Mar 24 Sep 24 Mar 25 Sep 25 Source: INEGI, Banxico and J.P. Morgan forecasts. Last update for Banxico March 21, 2024

Even further, with the modest upward revision to the inflation path (Figure 1)—for both headline and core—we believe the bank is implicitly incorporating that there will be some bumps in the road without jeopardizing the convergence to target. Of course, the caution and vigilance from the board is as usual, present. Upside risks to inflation remain on, while the COPOM is sidelining the recent growth disappointments for early 2024 data (see below), underscoring a firm labor market.

The slowdown in activity that is materializing earlier than expected should also pave the way to a more dovish narrative sooner rather than later, particularly if this starts to exert some pressure on the downside in core gauges.

With our US economists expecting the Fed to start easing in June (Figure 2) we believe Banxico has an important cushion to move ahead.

Figure 2: Banxico vs. Fed policy rate outlook %
![](images/e977315672a1b267cfc4d9aa944268395efaeb751e0d6a30ef0ff8ff624926d2.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan forecasts. Updated March 29(Banxico) and March 19(FOMC)

## January blues for economic activity

We had warned that following weakness in retail sales (and wholesales) and service-sector real income during January, the outlook for this week<sup>’</sup>s GDP proxy was worse than our initial forecast (+0.4%m/m). In the event, the release was even starker, with activity plunging 0.6%m/m in January, leaving output in that month more than 3.5% annualized below the 4Q level.

J.P. Morgan<sup>’</sup>s Nowcast had been signaling a large downside risk to our forecast for 1Q growth (originally 3%ar before this week<sup>’</sup>s data), but we held to the idea that services, which are underrepresented in our tracker, would gather steam after a weak spell in 4Q23. Again, the outcome was very weak, with services down 0.5%m/m. Finally, agriculture output didn<sup>’</sup>t help by plunging almost 13%m/m (Figure 3) as a harsh drought continues to bite on extremely low water supply.

Data releases and forecasts Week of March 25 - 29
Figure 3: GDP proxy by sector Index, sa, 2018=100
![](images/90c6a7a9df386cb3056bb5cf9d5a9181b107669514f5d93831d8aeb4cf91091b.jpg)

We continue to think that the underlying momentum of the economy is not as dire as depicted in the recent string of monthly GDP data but clearly it is not as robust as we expected. It seems highly unlikely that activity grows at all in 1Q. Thus, we are revising our 1Q forecast to -0.5%ar followed by a sharp public-spending led rebound (4.5%ar) in 2Q. Carryover from a weak 1Q leaves us forecasting full-year growth at 1.9%y/y instead of our earlier 2.3%.

## 1H Mar CPI unveils even stickier services

Headline inflation for 1H March came in close to expectations at 0.27%2w/2w, or 4.48%oya. However, this masks a somewhat larger surprise in core prices, which were up 0.33%2w/2w (vs. 0.27% expected), and a negative mix within core inflation itself.

Core goods printed a smaller-than-expected increase and are tracking modest gains of 0.3%m/m, sa in March and 3.4%ar in 1Q. This was the bright spot. By contrast, services prices increased across the board. They seem to have been up 0.5%m/m, sa in March (judging by this week<sup>’</sup>s preliminary data), and a large 5.6%ar in the quarter. We warned, however, that the shifting seasonality of Easter holidays would overstate this week<sup>’</sup>s number through its impact on airfares. Indeed, airfares skyrocketed about 35% in the first two weeks of March. We expect this to be mostly unwound in April.

But to us the real source of concern is that if we take the tourism component out of the equation from “other services inflation (services ex. housing and education), leaving “nontourism services<sup>”</sup>, things do not look better, and this metric is much less noise-free than broader services inflation measures. Non-tourism services prices jumped 0.6%m/m, sa, roughly the same as in the preceding three months. Thus, the smoothed 3m/3m annualized rate ballooned to 6.94%ar, roughly double the historical norm. As we have pointed out before, the stickiness in services prices (Figure 4) does not only reflect cyclical forces (which we think are not as weak as current data imply), but also structural ones, particularly rapidly rising real wages due to policy changes.

The uncomfortable combination of stagnant growth and core stickiness will continue to paint a complex picture for Banxico, but considering the level of monetary restriction in the economy (the above-7% ex-ante policy rate and the overvalued currency) we are comfortable with our new call for backto-back cuts.

![](images/ea57e4aa587b71d4aac5f6237cc609aa3edeb2f65b016da77c350e74d94a95a6.jpg)

## Brazil

Despite more dovish Fed, BCB decision and change of guidance were in line with our expectations

Sixth 50bp cut was accompanied by a shortening of the forward guidance

Despite the more hawkish change, data will dictate the terminal rate, which we still see at 9.5%

Fiscal revenues started the year strong, postponing the debate around the fiscal target

Last week we highlighted that COPOM<sup>’</sup>s discussion would be less on pace and more on signaling—which we thought would bring a change to the forward guidance—and that the marginal fiscal data pointed to an uneventful bimonthly review this Friday. Indeed, BCB<sup>’</sup>s board unanimously voted for the sixth 50bp cut as widely expected, and the COPOM decided to shorten the guidance and for now commit to only a 50bp cut in May, beyond which the decisions will be more data dependent.

The COPOM also smoothed the message by saying that the guidance changed due to more uncertainty, highlighting that their baseline scenario remains unchanged in spite of strong economic activity growth and higher-than-expected inflation lately. In this sense, this week<sup>’</sup>s decision and message seemed broadly consistent with our terminal rate of 9.50% as, beyond the May meeting, we expect two additional cuts, one of 50bp followed by one of 25bp before ending a 425bp cycle.

Meanwhile, the fiscal news were more constructive. If tax collections were the only guide, indeed the economic team would have nothing but reasons to celebrate. According to Brazil<sup>’</sup>s IRS, total tax collection reached BRL186.5bn in February, growing over 12%oya in real terms. While authorities highlight some non-recurring revenues on its records, including the payment of deferred taxes on closed investment vehicles, the improvement is also noticeable for recurring revenues (Figure 1).

Past revenues are no guarantee of future surpluses, but they were enough to avoid spending constraints in 2024<sup>’</sup>s first bimonthly fiscal review. Considering recent data and official forecasts, the government announced temporary cuts of only BRL2.9bn —below the estimates between BRL5 and BRL15bn that appeared in headlines, and a far cry from the BRL25.9bn parameter that Finance Ministry argues should be the cap for expenditure cuts for the 2024 fiscal year.

Figure 1: Federal revenue and activity index 3mma oya%, real
![](images/5166b1ce6c1f6ae38144d62ea996562031db936a07d6abe16b06396c59b13ee8.jpg)
Source: RFB, BCB and J.P. Morgan

A more challenging path, however, lies ahead. The government still anticipates important revenue flows from what we see as uncertain sources, such as tax litigation. Expenditures on social security appear to be underbudgeted (Table 1), and will pressure discretionary spending unless the government gets the green light to increase up to BRL15bn as per the extraordinary revenue trigger created by the new fiscal framework. Unless it chooses to change the 2025 target for a 0.5% surplus next year, the economic team must deliver the 2025 budget guidelines by April 15. Unless they opt to change the target, a move that could spark concerns and a round of negative revisions for next year, they will have to design a game plan to improve fiscal results by over 1% of GDP in our view.

## Growth acceleration in January

Following the better-than-expected retail sales and services output, the January economic activity index (IBC-Br) grew 0.6%m/m, sa (Figure 2). The December-January performance was the strongest since the beginning of last year, leading IBC-Br to grow 3.6%3m/3m, saar through January, which leaves a carryover of almost 5% to 1Q growth.

Following this result, the nowcaster moved further up to 4.5%, well above our call for a 2.4%q/q, saar expansion in 1Q GDP (Figure 3). We recognize the upside risk, but keep our above-consensus GDP growth forecasts unchanged as we expect part of the strength of the economy to be offset by weakening agricultural output.

## Argentina

• 4Q23 GDP plunged by 7.3q/q, saar...

• … and activity is poised to continue falling in 1H24

• A new monthly fiscal surplus, yet relying on real spending contraction

• The nature of the spending adjustment poses challenges ahead

## 4Q23 GDP plunged, as expected

The 4Q23 national accounts report showed sequential GDP plunging by 7.3%q/q, saar, roughly in line with what the monthly economic activity indicator suggested. Following revisions, GDP thus closed the year down 1.6%y/y. Last year was marked by the drought that decimated agricultural production in 2Q23, when GDP contracted by 10.5%q/q, saar, and by the regime change in late 4Q23. All in, GDP closed the year 2.2% below the peak observed in 1Q23. And there is more downside ahead.

The demand side breakdown showed domestic demand plunging in 4Q23, spearheaded by investment and public consumption (down by 11% and 6%ar). On its side, private sector consumption dropped by a more moderate 2%ar. Regarding net exports, the ill-suited policy mix underpinned by the appreciation of the real official exchange rate compounded by the dramatic drought wreaking havoc on crop production came to an end in 4Q. Real imports thus plunged 10%ar. But so did exports, which contracted at a 7% annualized pace.

![](images/2d49ffaa2dc26540daafbca417d9c08975c7968269aec33550747e844382d5d3.jpg)

Amid regime change, activity is poised to drop further in 2024, the costs associated with purging prior macro sins, to rein on inflation and set the basis for sustained growth ahead. Throughout 2022-23, we depicted the secular trend as the “great stagflation<sup>”</sup>. The data speak clearly. By the end of 2023, real GDP printed 4.1% below the 2017 peak. Indeed, the stagnation regime has lasted for more than 10 years (Figure 1). Note that real GDP has increased only 9.9% since pre-2008 Global Financial Crisis levels. That translates into negative per capita GDP growth.

The recalibration of the official FX by the end of 2023, together with the correction of relative prices, prompted inflationary acceleration, the real erosion of income and money aggregates amid very negative real rates, and the draconian fiscal adjustment to which the government is committed. The confluence of these three factors will likely require a severe adjustment in domestic demand, particularly in 1H24. In fact, early (and partial) data suggest activity is slipping by more than 6%oya in 1Q24. Yet, the recovery of agricultural production, which subtracted close to 2%-pts from 2023 annual GDP, is likely to offset in part the headline activity loss, starting in the second quarter.

Against this backdrop, we maintain our forecast for sequential growth to prove negative in the first half of the year, recovering by the second half on the assumption that the stabilization effort proves successful, with GDP growing at a 4.0%ar average pace. This path would be consistent with annual GDP at -3.6%y/y.

![](images/7eb113947fb3528589f100938f924a547dbfe072d029adf3acc847293f39865f.jpg)
Source: INDEC and J.P. Morgan

## A new monthly fiscal surplus relying on real spending plunge

The Treasury reported a new primary surplus in February of US\$1.4bn equivalent (0.3% of our 1Q24 annualized GDP estimate), the second in a row. The fiscal consolidation thus remains on track, with the YTD primary surplus reaching 0.8% of GDP, as compared to the 0.4% of GDP shortfall reported in the first two months of 2023.

At the same time time, the headline balance (including interest payments) posted a 0.2% of GDP surplus YTD, the first seen for two consecutive months since the beginning of 2011. This represents a sizeable fiscal consolidation when compared to the 0.9% of GDP shortfall in the same period of 2023. On a last-12-month basis, the headline balance is running at a 0.1% surplus, compared to a 3% shortfall at yearend (Figure 3).

Similar to January, the fiscal consolidation relied on a contraction in primary expenditures (-38%oya), while primary

Trade balance

Week of March 25 - 29

revenues remained in the red (-6.2%oya). On the revenue side, the decline in primary revenues was mainly driven by lower income tax collection (-35.6%oya) and social security revenues (-23.9%oya), despite higher tax collection arising from foreign-trade and “PAIS<sup>”</sup> taxes (see our recent note). On the spending side, the plunge in primary spending was again spearheaded by social transfers (-29.6%oya), which represent 63% of total primary spending. Note that the real erosion of social spending amid inflation acceleration drove pension spending in February to the lowest level in real terms since April 2007. In all, 44% of the YTD spending adjustment was explained by the real decline in social transfers, 26% by capital spending and provincial transfers, another 15% by subsidies, and the remainder by consumption spending and other.

The nature of the spending adjustment imposes challenges ahead. The race to the bottom of revenues and spending is not sustainable over time, as political and social restrictions eventually would become binding. In other words, the fact that a sizable portion of the spending adjustment relies on the real erosion of social spending (and, to a lesser extent, payments delays) sets a limit to the pace of the ongoing fiscal consolidation. Thus, an economic activity rebound in 2H24 is necessary for the fiscal consolidation program to succeed. That would in turn require the government to change the nature of the fiscal adjustment as the disinflation trend consolidates. For this to pan out, a political agreement that allows the government to secure additional revenues as well as move forward some key reforms, particularly related to pension and labor, would likely be needed, in our view.

Against this backdrop, we now watch for the new omnibus bill 2.0 to reach Congress in the coming week, with the voting expected to take place in April. From a fiscal perspective, the most relevant changes are related to a new pension adjustment formula, as well as a separate fiscal relief package that includes new revenues from tax amnesty and the re-incorporation of the income tax (both measures jointly accounting for about 0.8% of GDP in new revenues in our estimates). We forecast a sizable fiscal consolidation this year, though only enough to achieve a primary fiscal balance, consistent with a -1.7% of GDP headline result.

![](images/9dac04c9809fc9e84e42d75de0999295860164ee67ee1a00f2e6d13964effc96.jpg)

## Andeans

• Chile: 4Q23 national accounts showed private consumption reviving...

• … though investment continued to trend lower

• Colombia: Primary production started the year with a bang...

• … prompting us to revise up 2024 GDP growth to 1.6%y/y

## Chile: Consumer spending revives

The national accounts showed real GDP growth a bit softer in 4Q23, up by just 0.4%ar, compared to the 0.8% suggested by the monthly activity indicator. But we would argue against the idea of a weaker stance, as the adjustment lower for 4Q owed to an upward revision to 3Q23. More relevant, in our view, is to look at the revision to 4Q/4Q when adjusting for seasonality and calendar effects. This metric was revised higher, to +0.74%oya, from 0.53%. Therefore, all revisions included, the economy gained an extra 20bp in activity growth.

Parsing the sequential performance of the economy from the expenditure side (Figure 1) shows a revival in household consumption and a continued fall in fixed investment. On the consumption side, household consumption gained 1.7%ar, after revisions ending a sequence of six quarters of consecutive contractions. Indeed, private consumption ended the year at -8.9%,sa, below the cyclical peak observed in 1Q22. Yet, it still stands 12.3% above the level recorded in 4Q19. On the other side, public sector consumption slipped in the quarter, down by 1.2%ar. But different from household consumption, public sector consumption has experienced an upward trend, currently +25.2% above the level observed back in 4Q19. Aggregated consumption gained a still feeble 1.3%ar. We expect consumption to firm up in the quarters ahead, aided by disinflation, some gains in the labor market, and a still expansive fiscal policy.

Figure 1: Chile consumption performance Index, sa, 1Q20=100
![](images/066943f910273a1351acb82707f1e8bee156bcc551bdd7b80b758c19df955f8d.jpg)

The gross investment blues continue. Gross fixed investment contracted by 13.3%ar, on the back of -8.9%ar in 3Q. The extremely poor capex performance continues to weigh not only on overall activity, but also on potential growth ahead. Indeed, by the end of 2023, investment was basically as flat as in 4Q19. The narrative aligns with both construction and M&E. Both contracted in 4Q23, and both are basically flat with respect to 4Q19 levels. In fact, the relative performance of public sector consumption to investment should eventually gain relevance in a discussion of secular trends (see Figure 2 for a long-term comparison of the relative dynamics).

Finally, net exports contributed positively in 4Q23. But that should not mask the fact that exports dropped by 3.5%ar, against imports, which fell 8.7%ar. Imports are running just 4% above the pre-social unrest levels, which together with the marginal consumption gains suggest we may be reaching a plateau in the coming two quarters. On the exports side, mining volumes continue to prove a drag, but that is not the only reason behind the secular stagnation.

In all, the annual average real GDP growth closed 2023 in the black, by +0.2%y/y (consistent with the aforementioned 4Q/4Q revision higher). Ex-mining GDP gained +0.3%y/y, while mining lost -0.3%y/y. Yet, the revisions did bring a notso-positive consequence, by lowering the statistical carryover, though by a tad. Indeed, the statistical carryover calculated on a quarterly basis logged at +0.3%-pt, which compares to the prior +0.4%-pt estimate. But the lower carryover gets offset by the January activity performance, so annual GDP growth remains 2.0%y/y, consistent with 2.6% 4Q/4Q (nsa).

Figure 3: Chile Fixed investment and public sector consumption Index, sa, 1Q20=100
![](images/22eb1c34c522667e7ba9f13a8fa6dbf9740e6ae521cf2a92a0bb965231ef1a74.jpg)

## Colombia: Kicking the year off with a bang

The monthly activity index reported by DANE showed a stronger-than-expected activity performance in January, explained basically by a leap higher of primary production.

Headline activity gained by 2.2%m/m, sa, printing above our expectation for +0.7%. This proved the third consecutive monthly gain, coming on the heels of +0.2% in December. Thus, the 3m/3m sequential activity pace accelerated to

5.1%saar. On an annual basis, activity recorded a +1.6%oya (nsa).

The main surprise on the month was the performance of primary production, which skyrocketed by 8.1%m/m, sa in the first month of the year. Primary sectors are thus growing at a +10.1%3m/3m, saar pace, showing an upward trend since late 2022. On the other side, secondary and tertiary production remain sluggish. Secondary slipped by 0.5%m/m, sa, while tertiary posted +0.2%m/m, sa. On a sequential basis, growth rates are positive, though fairly low (+0.4% and +2.0%3m/3m, saar, respectively).

On a trend basis, the monthly jump has helped the ISE level to converge back to the the levels suggested by the extrapolation of pre-COVID trend growth (Figure 3).

In terms of forecasts, while acknowledging the potential reversal of the Jan<sup>’</sup>24 primary production leap in coming months, its magnitude prompts a revision higher of 1Q24 GDP, to 4.0%q/q, saar (from 1.0%). In addition, El Niño has proven more moderate than expected, easing our concern about potential spikes in food prices with eventual downside impact on consumption and activity. Therefore, we feel comfortable revising higher 2024 annual GDP growth by 60bp, to +1.6%y/y. This would be consistent with 2.8%4Q/4Q, a level the BanRep<sup>’</sup>s Staff deems as potential GDP for the current year.

Figure 3: Colombia economic activity tracker Index, sa, 2015 =100

## Chile

## United Kingdom

Slightly softer inflation in Feb, but persistence measures remain firm

• Flash PMI still pointing to a growth recovery in 1Q

• BoE rate unchanged, but hawkish dissents dropped

• We still forecast an August cut, but risks tilt earlier

The BoE remained on hold this week but the main surprise was the two hawkish dissenters dropping their votes for higher rates. This has raised expectations for a June cut, while sparking a conversation about May. There is limited data prior to the May meeting, and with the consensus-voting members expressing a range of views this will probably serve to slow the formation of a majority in favour of a cut. A lot more data will be available before June. But with slow progress on services inflation and upside risks to growth - again highlighted by this week<sup>’</sup>s data – the BoE is unlikely to rush and will need more convincing. We note that the hawks removed their dissents this week with relatively little new information, and so there is a risk that the BoE<sup>’</sup>s reaction function again lurches unexpectedly towards an earlier cut. We retain our August call, but recognize the risks have tilted towards an earlier move.

## Small downside surprise to inflation

Headline CPI prices rose 0.1% m/m, sa in February, based on our seasonal adjustment, taking the year-ago rate down from 4.0% to 3.4% (J.P.Morgan, Consensus and BoE: 3.5). Core prices also rose 0.2%m/m, sa taking the year-ago rate down from 5.1% to 4.5% (J.P.Morgan and Consensus 4.6%). Services inflation fell from 6.5% to 6.1%oya (J.P.Morgan 6.2; Consensus 6.0; BoE 6.1). RPI inflation fell from 4.9% to 4.5%oya (J.P.Morgan 4.5; Consensus 4.4).

Relative to our forecast, the small downside surprise on the CPI came from both core goods and services. Using our adjustment, we estimate core goods prices fell 0.1%m/m, sa, matching the prior month and a small surprise given the deflation trend appeared to be nearing an end. The PMI has been signalling a manufacturing pricing shift, but that is more on the input side. Audio visual goods and other recreational goods were weak over the month, partly offset by a gain in clothing. On services, we estimate prices rose 0.3%m/m, sa, another softer print. Unlike last month<sup>’</sup>s airfare-induced weakness, this time communication prices stood out with a decline, but there were a few small misses compared to our forecast elsewhere, including transport equipment, catering and accommodation.

The BoE<sup>’</sup>s persistence-weighted measure of services inflation excludes accommodation, as well as airfares and non-private rents. We estimate this came out at 0.4%m/m, sa in February, a touch softer than its 0.5% average from the prior six months (using our seasonal adjustment). Overall, though, this measure is still running firm with only limited signs of slowing. We estimate the 3m/3m annualised rate was 5.5% in February, similar to a couple of months ago. This is a notable contrast to core goods inflation, which is running at -1.5% on a comparable basis (Table 1).

In other data, XpertHR<sup>’</sup>s latest pay settlement report showed a small decline in median pay settlements for the three months to February, from 5.0% (revised from 5.1%) down to 4.8%. The mean was down from 5.2% to 5.0%. This measure remains high but is moving in the right direction for the BoE. XpertHR has also surveyed the organisations it collects data from, and employers forecast a median pay award of 4% for 2024. This is low compared to forward-looking surveys from both the BoE and DMP, which have come in above 5%, but if realised it would remove some of the upside risk around the BoE<sup>’</sup>s wage forecast for this year.

While headline inflation is running a little below the BoE<sup>’</sup>s expectations as of February, the much focused-on services category was in line with its February projections at 6.1%oya, limiting the policy implications from this release. Our latest forecast is shown in the second table below. It includes the latest announcement on energy bills falling 12% in April, and the latest weights update to the RPI. The weight on the strong running categories has gone up (e.g. services) while that on the falling categories has declined (e.g. energy), which pushes up our forecast vs. our prior assumptions. The ONS also made methodological changes to the collection of second hand cars and private rents, as described here. The impact on the CPI going forward, however, is estimated to be worth less than +0.1% on an annual basis.

## Flash PMI shows small decline

The flash PMI in March showed a small decline from 53.0 to 52.9, essentially leaving the survey broadly unchanged and at a level that continues to comfortably signal a return to positive growth at the start of this year. Based on a model of the past relationship with GDP, the current level of the PMI points to growth of over 1% annualised. This relationship broke down in 2023, however, leaving more uncertainty about how to read the exact pace of growth. The PMI raises a question about whether recent GDP out-turns will be revised higher over time. Either way, the positive momentum swing in the PMI in recent months is hard to ignore. We continue to expect GDP will show a return to positive growth in 1Q (1% annualised) with some upside risk. The details showed limited changes, although there was a clear two-point step up in manufacturing output to 50.2 to offset a moderate decline in services. Both manufacturing pricing components firmed and there was a large five-point jump in orders to 50.1. The composite employment reading ticked down to 50.0, signalling modest job growth, and future output expectations remained very high in March, despite giving back some of the prior month<sup>’</sup>s gain (Figure 1).

## BoE keeps rates and guidance unchanged

The BoE left rates unchanged at 5.25% at its March meeting, with the main surprise coming from the vote. Both hawkish dissents were dropped, leaving an 8-1 vote with one dissent for a cut (Dhingra). On one level, it is no surprise to see the hawks remove their votes for higher rates, but the exact timing does seem odd.

The statement contained no changes in forward guidance, maintaining a neutral stance, while macroeconomic data developments since February were mixed with few significant surprises. Growth prospects have improved somewhat, as fiscal measures take effect and business surveys flag an improvement. And while there were further signs of progress on inflation, wage growth and a loosening in the labour market, the MPC stressed ongoing tightness and relatively slow progress on the key measures it is focussing on. In the statement, it noted that “key indicators of inflation persistence remain elevated.<sup>”</sup>

The drop in hawkish dissents shows the direction of travel on the MPC, and signals that the time for a rate cut is drawing nearer. It tilts the risks around our call for an August cut towards an earlier move. But with no material changes in the MPC<sup>’</sup>s broader communications, no additional votes for a cut, and limited data available before the next meeting, May still looks too early.

June is live and a realistic possibility. A lot of data is available before that meeting. But the persistence measures the MPC is watching need to come off more quickly, and upside growth risks could yet cause renewed caution on the MPC. The minutes note that there is still a wide range of views among those that didn<sup>’</sup>t dissent, and so greater clarity on the data is still required in order for a majority to form in favour of a cut. We continue to expect 75bps of cumulative cuts by year end.

## Sweden and Norway

• Norges Bank guiding for autumn cut...

• ...despite much lower expected core inflation

• Riksbank likely to guide for a 2Q cut next week

Swedish core prices rebounded 0.6%m/m (nsa, sa: 0.2%) in February after declining in January. The over-year-ago rate dropped 0.9%-pt to 3.5%oya, in line with our call but below consensus (3.6%) and the Riksbank (3.7%). Details were mixed with services ex. housing rebounding 0.6%m/m (sa) whilst core goods prices were down 0.2%m/m, sa. However, some of the services firmness, such as in transport services, looks temporary, in our view. Our measure of wage-sensitive inflation and volatiles were close to flat on the month and our measure of FX-sensitive inflation rose just 0.1% in February.

Overall, the report should be dovish for the Riksbank, with all three measures of inflation coming in below projections. While the high monthly increase in services prices is still a concern, momentum remains benign and various price indica tors suggest core inflation should soon hit the 2% target (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Core inflation and industry' price expectations
![](images/d4dfe268d2fd34dddd683d28023eb2b67bb90b010f0229fd2165eaddb8991e0a.jpg)

Next week, the Riksbank should keep the policy rate on hold, but guide for a cut in May and/or June. Our call is for May, as the inflation picture looks benign, the SEK is stronger than projected, and the Fed/ECB are guiding for easing in June. Moreover, Sweden is one of the few places in DM experiencing a “real<sup>”</sup> recession with falling GDP and a rising unemployment rate. Recent speeches have also been very dovish. Otherwise, the calendar is thin in the coming weeks. We expect a touch higher composite PMI, driven by manufacturing. This would roughly align with our forecast of flat growth in 1Q.

## Norges Bank not so data-dependent

Norges Bank kept the policy rate on hold at 4.5% at the March meeting, as widely expected. The Board guided for a cut in Autumn in the statement, with the Governor clarifying this as September. The rate path was little changed compared to December, despite the 2024 core inflation forecast being slashed 0.7%. This reflected concerns of NOK depreciation. As mentioned in our preview, such hawkish guidance would push back our call of a first cut till September. Beyond September, we look for a cut in December, followed by four more in 2025. This is 60bps lower than Norges Bank<sup>’</sup>s rate path, which is justified by our lower wage and inflation forecast. We also think Norges Bank<sup>’</sup>s focus on the NOK will gradually fade as major central banks start to cut rates.

![](images/c045d4ddd94f981b0df17d8d10189815f9f51a5d6906762316a9e6be44546595.jpg)

Core prices increased 0.4%m/m in February (0.0%m/m, sa), leading to a 0.4%-pt drop in the year-ago rate to 4.9%oya. This was 0.2%-pt below our call, which was the lowest amongst surveyed economists and well below Norges Bank<sup>’</sup>s forecast of 5.5%. Indeed, this is consistent with our long-held view of downside risks to the bank<sup>’</sup>s 2024 inflation projections. While food prices were a significant driver of the undershoot, other domestic goods and services were also weak as well.

The TBU Committee presented its revised forecast for CPI inflation in 2024, which remained unchanged at 4.1%oya. This is the most important data point for the wage talks. The TBU saw upside risks to the first estimate from higher electricity price futures and the weak NOK, however, the big downward surprise in February CPI offset this.

Norges Bank<sup>’</sup>s 1Q24 Regional Network Survey showed the output indicator for the current quarter improving from -0.3 to -0.1%, while the next quarter estimate was flat. Wage growth for 2024 was revised up 0.4%-pt to 4.9%. Capacity and labour supply constraints were unchanged, below their historical averages. Employment growth edged slightly higher, anticipated to rise 0.1%q/q on average in 1H24.

To some extent mirroring the RN Survey, Norway mainland GDP rebounded 0.4%m/m, sa, in January, above expectations (JPM: 0.2%, consensus: 0.1%, Norges Bank: -0.2%). However, details were poor, with household consumption and investment declining. The outcome implies that our 1Q24 GDP forecast of 0.5%q/q, saar, is still within reach.

Hold deposit rate at 4.50%

## Emerging Europe

• Türkiye: CBRT surprises with 500bps hike to 50%

• Czech Republic: CNB cuts 50bps, as expected

• Poland: IP still sluggish, real wage growth surges

• Israel: Inflation momentum firmed in February

• Russia: CBR on hold, signal remains neutral

## Türkiye: CBRT surprises with 500bps hike

The CBRT hiked the policy rate by 500bps to 50%, as opposed to our expectation and market consensus of an unchanged rate at 45% (Figure 1). The front-loaded rate hike was a response to the deterioration in the inflation outlook and decline in FX reserves (JPMe: 50% in April). The CBRT stated that tight monetary stance will be maintained until there is a significant and sustained decline in the underlying trend of monthly inflation, and until inflation expectations converge to the projected forecast range. We interpret this as the CBRT being in no rush to cut the policy rate as disinflation starts in 3Q24. We maintain our end-2024 policy rate forecast of 45% as we expect the CBRT to cut its policy rate in November and December by 250bps each. The forward guidance was also hawkish as the CBRT kept the door open for further rate hikes by stating that monetary policy stance will be tightened in case a significant and persistent deterioration in inflation is foreseen.

Figure 1: Policy rate and market rates
![](images/24d28c7b682a621e2417b8fcd11eac552f1fe213be222a4e0c7a5ab1fd734da0.jpg)

The CBRT widened the interest rate corridor, and set overnight lending rates at 53%. The CBRT adjusted the monetary policy operational framework by setting the overnight borrowing and lending rates 300 bps (previously ± 150bps) below and above the policy rate of 50%, respectively. It will allow the CBRT to tighten in the short-term as overnight lending rates might reach as high as 53% (previously 46.5%) if needed.

The CBRT highlighted recent measures announced in March to curb loan growth and increase Turkish lira deposit rates. The CBRT pledged additional macroprudential measures in case of unanticipated developments in credit growth and deposit rates. The CBRT is likely to be more sensitive to any fall in Turkish deposit rates, excess lira liquidity in the system, and the acceleration in loan growth going forward. Following macroprudential measures in March, the average deposit rate up to 3 months rose to 55.7%, consumer loan rate surged to 72%, and commercial loan rates reached 58.5% as of 15 March (Figure 1). We believe that elevated lira deposit rates are crucial for unwinding the FX-protected lira deposit scheme (KKM), and to stop rising dollarization among Turkish residents.

## Czech Republic: CNB 50bp cut as expected

This week the CNB board voted to cut the 2-week repo rate by 50bps to 5.75% (Figure 1), in line with our expectation, making it the lowest policy rate in CEE, on par with the NBP<sup>’</sup>s. Markets were pricing a near 50/50 chance of a 75bp move, which was not a totally misplaced view, as the vote split shows two board members (out of seven) voted for a 75bp cut (this was likely to be Deputy Governor Jan Frait, who already pushed for 75bps in February, and Tomáš Holub, who has also voiced openness to a speedier rate reduction).

Figure 1: CNB interest rate corridor
![](images/cabf81a29e8c959f61bfe51077f844207d29f5995037243566902065e7f11e01.jpg)

The statement maintains the usual hawkish inclination, and despite the downside surprises to headline CPI at the start of the year, the board sees “modestly inflationary risks in the outlook.<sup>”</sup> Given these risks, it indicates that policy must remain tight and further cuts will be approached with caution. The hawkish zenith is reached in the segment that suggests the easing cycle “can be paused or terminated at any time at levels that are still restrictive if inflation – especially its core component – does not develop in line with the forecast.<sup>”</sup> However hawkish, with the exception of the emphasis on core CPI, this is all repetition from February, so no “new<sup>”</sup> news here.

One aspect that is different is that the currency is explicitly mentioned as an upside risk to inflation, whereas in February that had not been mentioned. The press conference also brought no information in terms of guidance, but the Governor mentioned that the next quarterly projection in May will be accompanied by some analysis/study of the neutral rate, which can provide some hints about the terminal rate.

The 5-2 vote split is, in our view, a neutral outcome: it doesn<sup>’</sup>t suggest an acceleration to 75bp is obviously imminent (as a 4-3 split perhaps would), nor does it suggest an unexpected degree of hawkishness (as a 6-1 vote would imply). We remain of the view that the May decision (no meeting in April) will be another 50bp cut. On the data front, what will matter are the developments of the March inflation print together with the currency and any changes about expectations on ECB/Fed. Apart from the odd January print, inflation data has been showing stickiness in the disinflation process, especially at the core services level. As long as this persists, we think there will be no appetite for an acceleration in the easing pace. After May, the pace of cuts likely will slow to 25bp/meeting (although an extra 50bp in June is quite possi ble), and we see the policy rate ending the year at 4% (with some risks for a slightly lower rate).

## Poland: IP sluggish, real wages surge

Industrial production fell marginally in February by 0.1%m/m sa, extending the soft patch of the previous quarters (Figure 2). The output declines of 1H23 were roughly offset in 2H23, leaving the level of production roughly at the same level as a year ago (+0.3%oya, after we account for working-day differences). On a 3m/3m basis, there is still a positive impulse, with output expanding at around a 1.7% annualized pace.

Figure 2: Polish industrial production
![](images/a8b0a233530f9d76258d35856a26d08061ef39ac7cef1ead0a8b846cb9ff0060.jpg)
Source: GUS, J.P. Morgan

Separately, the average wage in the private sector reached PLN7,978 in February, which is up 12.9% from a year ago (Figure 3), marginally up from the 12.8% recorded in January. Wage growth is promoted by a tight labour market as well as a large 17.8% rise in the statutory minimum wage at the start of the year. Salaries are increasing the most in construction (15.6%oya), administrative. and support functions (14.8%oya), water supply, sewage, waste (14.5%oya) and manufacturing (14.1%), but is generally spread across all sectors. Wage growth stickiness combined with the deep decline in inflation, results in a surge in real wage growth to 9.6%oya, the highest in the recorded series. At this point, real wages have recovered all the loss from the 2022/23 inflation surge, and are at an historical high. If we combine this with increases in social spending that took place this year, namely the updating of the PLN500 child-support scheme into a PLN800 version, households are benefitting from the biggest increase in real incomes in modern history .

Figure 3: Private sector wage growth
![](images/da29b1ac86445b928c3614d86c9dff91f8a1d81137aa4ff0ca852136634f3254.jpg)

## Israel: Inflation momentum firmed

Consumer inflation slowed to 2.5%oya in February, from 2.6% in March, a tad less than we had anticipated (JPMe 2.4%, consensus 2.5%). Sequential momentum in headline CPI firmed to 2.1%m/m, saar, but in core remained soft at \~1.2% thanks to a fall in shelter component.

The surprise to our expectations was concentrated in the goods segment – momentum in food (6.1%m/m saar), energy (12.5) and core goods (2.5) was stronger than anticipated and above the targeted 2%. Although we did expect the impact of shipment disruptions to add to goods inflation, the effect has been stronger (or more front-loaded) than we thought. With that said, a gradual recovery in port calls and maritime import volumes seen in recent weeks suggests that by summer the shipments-related price shock should be largely over. The dynamics in global energy and agricultural prices also suggest that local price increases should not be protracted.

Services inflation surprised to the downside, with sequential momentum easing to an estimated 0.5%m/m, saar. Shelter inflation was the main drag at -1.4%m/m, saar, while much of the rest of services, including the recovering recreation and entertainment segments, showed notable increases. House prices returned to growth in late 2023, which will probably lead to a reversal in imputed rent dynamics later in 2024.

Estimated median inflation momentum (across 115 components) firmed to 3.2%m/m, saar, from 1.6% in previous month. The broad-based firming in price momentum probably reflects the impacts of both supply disruptions and a pick up in (pent-up) domestic demand. Looking forward, we anticipate price pressures in tradables to ease in next 2-3 months, but non-tradables to pick up some pace, with overall inflation momentum averaging just above targeted 2% in the rest of the year.

Figure 6: CPI ex. food and energy vs. median inflation - Israel
![](images/5bf20230f4fe9ccb73e96120dd173144158d4f7f36d22e018027dcfa3ced7b4e.jpg)

The February CPI report arguably vindicates the BoI<sup>’</sup>s caution at the last policy meeting, but nevertheless leaves space for continued gradual monetary policy easing during the year. We expect the BoI to cut by a total of 75bp in the rest of the year, but admit that risks now are probably skewed toward fewer cuts in this cycle, especially as we now expect fewer Fed cuts. Fiscal policy remains the main constraint for deeper monetary policy easing, we believe, both due to the large positive impulse to domestic demand this year and due to the impact on pricing via increases in indirect taxes next year (even as fiscal impulse should turn negative then). We expect inflation to slow to around 2.0-2.2%oya by 3Q24, before bouncing back to 2.4% by end-2024. In 2025, we expect inflation to rise to about 2.8% on the back of VAT hike (we assume \~70% pass-through).

## Russia: CBR on hold

The CBR left the key rate unchanged at 16.0% at Friday<sup>’</sup>s meeting, as widely expected. The language of the statement did not change much, as the economy still runs above potential and inflation momentum did not change much in recent weeks. The signal remained neutral, with the board guiding for a protracted period of elevated policy rate to secure disinflation toward 4%. Assessment of risks also has not changed much, with the board seeing pro-inflationary risks – elevated inflation expectations, exchange rate risks amid unstable external conditions, further overheating of the economy, slow fiscal consolidation – prevailing on the outlook horizon.

With that said, Governor Nabiullina noted that the board had an active discussion about the appropriate policy path at the past meeting, with some board members believing that inflation momentum is already low enough to start easing (but others convinced that inflation risks are too high). Besides, the Governor mentioned that the CBR will be ready to ease policy once it gains confidence that the disinflation process is entrenched. For now, neither inflation nor growth and labor market data grant such confidence. Minutes from prior meeting also suggested that appropriate degree of tightness may be achieved with lower rate once inflation starts slowing, while officials<sup>’</sup> comments on Friday suggested that the CBR expects inflation to peak in 2Q24.

Our sense is that by the April meeting the balance in the board will start shifting in a more dovish direction and, although a change in policy rate already at the next meeting looks quite unlikely, CBR<sup>’</sup>s signalling is likely to soften, opening the door to rate cuts in either the June or July meetings. Our base case remains for the first cut to be delivered in July. We continue to expect a total of 500bp in cuts in 2H24, but admit that risks now appear increasingly skewed toward more cautious easing, as the economy continues to prove more resilient to shocks, including the substantial monetary tightening of last year.

## South Africa

Inflation a tenth higher than expected at 5.6%oya in Feb

• Large health insurance hikes underpin the miss

• We see SARB staying on hold next week at 8.25%...

• ...with the easing cycle now likely only commencing in Sep (May previously)

Headline Inflation picked up to 5.6% in February, from 5.3% in January, a tenth ahead of our and consensus expectations, but inflation has now likely peaked and will drift marginally lower near term. Most of the 1%m/m rise in the February CPI Index came from the miscellaneous services category, mainly insurances services, with the remaining 0.2%pt of the m/m CPI gain due to higher transportation costs. Core inflation rose to 5.0%oya, from 4.6% in January, as the small decline in core goods inflation to 5% was insufficient to counter the rise in services inflation to 4.9% (from 4%).

March 25 - 29
Figure 1: Medical insurance and core inflation
![](images/356339a37048bebf2918dcf0a2a0fcbfb2278032589069f4c0a284714b1610b3.jpg)

Medical aid premiums were hiked by a substantive 10.3%m m in February, as part of the annual reviews to be up 12.9%oya (JPMe: 11.2%oya), from 6.9%oya in January (Figure 1). We expect no further increases during the remainder of the year, which should pull down over-year-ago medical insurance inflation to 10.3%oya from April on base effects. Last year about a third of providers had postponed the annual increase to end-March (from typically end-January). The higher health insurance price hike accounts for our 0.1%pt miss on headline inflation. We think the shifting seasonality, which now is reverting to pre-pandemic patterns, is temporarily adding 0.2%pts to headline inflation and 0.4%pts to services inflation in February and March, before fading from April. Therefore, the February report appears somewhat worse than fundamentals.

We now push back the beginning of the policy rate easing cycle by two meetings to September, although we retain our terminal rate with 75bp in cumulative easing (Figure 2). After a prolonged period of subdued services inflation, averaging

just 4.0% in the last two years, this component jumped to 4.9% last month, its highest level since 2Q19. Sure, this is mostly due to health insurance costs and was exacerbated by the shifting timing of surveys, yet, we estimate the more front-loaded increases this year probably only lifted services inflation by an additional 0.4%pts (0.2%pts on headline). More likely, services inflation now probably will no longer settle below the inflation target mid-point, reducing policy makers<sup>’</sup> confidence that headline inflation will drop to 4.5% by 4Q24 (SARB: 4.4% in 4Q24).

Figure 2: South Africa inflation outlook
![](images/f7d75e46655f0381a6feb095aee837a1ae716996e014f8563104f9075b3ee2f7.jpg)

Moreover, we have recently amended our policy rate outlook for the US to three cuts this year, with global financial conditions continuing to be relevant for SA via the currency and widening current account deficit. Lastly, as we had flagged, as elections now fall on the May MPC meeting dates, and with the election outcome uncertain and potentially impacting on the currency, the scope for a first cut next quarter has reduced. We look for the SARB to pause next week, but now see a shallow easing cycle in September for a total of two cuts this year with a third cut pushed to 1Q25. This should take the policy rate to 7.50% by 1Q25, from 8.25% currently, although El Niño, global inflation dynamics in 2H24 and the currency performance post-election are key risks factors that could impact on the policy rate outlook.

## Australia and New Zealand

The RBA board left the cash rate on hold this week, but softened the guidance

• Australia<sup>’</sup>s unemployment rate fell to 3.7%, a sharp decline from January’s 4.1%

Labor data have been noisy of late, but employment and participation are now back on trend

• NZ GDP contracted 0.1% in 4Q23, below consensus and RBNZ expectations

The RBA board left the cash rate steady this week at 4.35%, as widely forecasted among economists. We had expected the board to maintain its warning that a further rate hike couldn't be ruled out, and this sentiment was retained, but in slightly less forceful terms: “the path...that will best ensure that inflation returns to target in a reasonable timeframe remains uncertain and the Board is not ruling anything in or out.<sup>”</sup>

The language regarding the labor market remains relatively hawkish in that conditions “remain tighter than is consistent with sustained full employment and inflation at target.<sup>”</sup> Since the new mandate is to maximize employment subject to the inflation target constraint, in practical terms, this seems to effectively rule out easing, unless a large shock were to render softness in the labor market a sure thing.

In our view, the guidance shift is not very significant for the policy outlook when it would still be consistent with another hike (which we don't think is necessary), and as the state of the labor market seemingly prohibits cuts. In the press conference, Governor Bullock didn<sup>’</sup>t engage with the idea that the board had shifted to neutral or abandoned its hiking bias, stating that the economy is on “the same path<sup>”</sup> as previously. The language changes seemingly reflect the economy being a bit more advanced on that path, though, in her view, the board is still “not confident enough to rule out further interest rate changes<sup>”</sup> (hikes).

Similarly, the Governor described risks as “finely balanced.<sup>”</sup> This stands in contrast to markets pricing a downside skew to rates within a few months. Responding to a hypothetical question on necessary conditions for rate cuts, Bullock argued the board would need to be “much more confident<sup>”</sup> of inflation returning to the band. Similarly, while most media questions regarded potential activity-based catalysts for easing, when asked whether she was worried about the economy, Bullock<sup>’</sup>s blunt response was “I<sup>’</sup>m worried about inflation.<sup>”</sup>

## Labor market strengthens considerably

Australia<sup>’</sup>s unemployment rate declined four-tenths, to 3.7% in February (J.P. Morgan: 3.9%, consensus: 4%, Figure 1), alongside a surge in employment (+116K) and a one-tenth uptick in the participation rate (66.7%). As noted last month, the labor data have been noisy of late, with the ABS noting that increased marginal attachment may be temporarily distorting parts of the survey. These wrinkles appear to have been ironed out, with employment reverting to the underlying trend path and participation realigning with the 12-month average.

February<sup>’</sup>s outcome pushes back on market pricing for rate cuts from mid-year and supports our view that the RBA will lag most other DM central banks in easing policy. At Tuesday<sup>’</sup>s press conference, Governor Bullock described the labor market as “slightly on the tight side,<sup>”</sup> given that the jobless rate was still considerably lower than the pre-pandemic benchmark.

Figure 1: Australia underemployment and unemployment rates % of labour force, both axes
![](images/17fc927da7487d52c659901e5d8b8a1ad6574a717dc0999f4f0353782d7f628d.jpg)

While the labor data can be volatile from month to month, and caution is required when interpreting any individual print, the unemployment rate is now meaningfully below the RBA<sup>’</sup>s June forecast (4.2%) and just two-tenths above the cycle low (June 2023). Ultimately, we think the inflation landscape will dictate the timing and magnitude of RBA rate cuts, though this week<sup>’</sup>s step down in unemployment lowers the probability of near-term easing, and we retain our forecast for the first cut to be delivered in 1Q25.

Looking ahead, our expectation is for slowing domestic activity to weigh on labor demand and impart a further upward bias on the jobless rate. We look for the unemployment rate to reach 4.3% by June 2023, with a move toward 4.5% forecast by year-end. This view is broadly aligned with the RBA<sup>’</sup>s forecast and the signal from the leading labor indicators.

## NZ in technical recession

NZ GDP was softer than expected in 4Q, contracting slightly (-0.1%q/q vs. JPMf: +0.2%q/q, consensus: +0.1%q/q). This result is below the RBNZ's already-downbeat forecast of 0% and puts the economy back into technical recession. In the tracking data, every category had been down, with the exception of net trade. The official details this week roughly fol-

lowed that template, though with a bit less weakness in consumption/investment, and bigger swings in trade and inventories.

Household consumption rose 0.5%q/q in 4Q, a relatively limp recovery after a quite deep contraction through the middle of the year, and household consumption fell in %oya terms (Figure 2). The mix was skewed to services (+0.5%q/q) and dura ble goods (+0.6%q/q), both of which followed aggregate consumption in failing to recoup previous declines. Households expenditure overseas firmed quite a bit (+7.2%q/q), which makes the significant pullback in imports (-2.9%q/q) all the more pronounced in the remainder, with the extended easing in goods demand (retail volumes are 8% below Dec-2021 levels) now also flowing through to a de-stocking dynamic (inventories/balancing item contributed -1.9%-pt).

The RBNZ should have high confidence that the easing in core inflation is genuine and will continue through this year. The hawkish repricing for potential hikes over January-February feels like a fever dream, and the leadership had never really engaged with it, with Governor Orr having argued in his last speech that core inflation matters most, not non-tradables, or services which reflect relative prices and will be influenced by strong immigration in particular.

With activity data weak and core inflation metrics annualizing in the target band recently, the path to cuts this year seems relatively straightforward. All that awaits is for the RBNZ rhetoric to fall into line. Officials have been reluctant to engage with the cut narrative for fear of pre-emptively easing financial conditions, though this just stores up potential for a more abrupt pivot when it does come (per the leadership's “white heron<sup>”</sup> metaphor - moving with conviction once a decision is made). We moved our long-held call for first cut out from May to August after the last meeting to balance risks around the low visibility of this decision process, though an earlier move remains plausible.

Figure 2: NZ household and government consumption %oya, truncated
![](images/c797b27e2dcaa4e2c1d1598703d4cc4a7486a7267c89727eadad7d1deaa2974d.jpg)

## Greater China

• China: A strong start in January-February activity

• Revising up current-quarter growth to 6.6%q/q saar

• Hong Kong: Unemployment rate unchanged; CPI inflation at 2.1%oya on LNY seasonal factors

• Taiwan: February export orders larger than expected

• Central bank delivered a surprise 12.5bp hike

China<sup>’</sup>s January-February activity generally beat expectations. Industrial production rose 7.0%oya (Figure 1), consistent with stronger-than-expected export data released earlier, and solid manufacturing activity such as manufacturing investment and industrial-use electricity production. This was related to the recent policy priority to promote innovation and industry upgrade, especially in the manufacturing sector. Despite continuous weakness in real estate investment (-9.0%oya), strong manufacturing investment (9.4%) and steady infrastructure investment (6.3%oya) jointly contributed to better-than-expected FAI growth of 4.2%oya.

![](images/3cdf6c48d9763a9d45b98fcb872f64120ef6f28dcb35553cc98591242306aee0.jpg)
Source: NBS, J.P. Morgan

On the consumption side, the service production index rose 5.8%oya and retail sales rose 5.5%oya in January-February. Our estimates show that average January-February retail sales were 2.4%m/m sa higher than the December level. In the breakdown, retail sales growth was led by catering, telecommunications, sports and entertainment and auto sales. Consumption and service activity growth is due in part to LNY seasonality and in part to a lingering reopening dividend, as the consumption recovery is still incomplete.

A cautious reading is the modest increase in the unemployment rate. The survey-based unemployment rate rose to 5.3% in February (vs. 5.1% in December) and ticked up to 5.1% in 31 cities (vs. 5.0% in December). This is perhaps due to uneven policy support and industry developments. The manufacturing sector has outperformed, but does not seem to create new jobs at the same pace. In contrast, some service sectors (e.g., education, Internet platforms, financial services, etc.) have been affected by regulatory headwinds in recent years,

and their employment outlook is still soft, even though the worst period of regulatory headwinds might be behind us.

Not surprisingly, the weakest link in the economy remains the housing sector. Real estate investment declined 9.0%oya in January-February (vs. -9.6% in 2023). New residential home sales declined 24.8% in volume terms and fell 32.7%oya in value terms, and house price declines widened in January-February. Housing-related consumption items also stayed weak, e.g., construction and decoration (2.1%oya) and furniture (4.6%oya) sales.

## Revising up 1Q24 growth forecast

Stronger-than-expected activity in January-February led us to revise up our current-quarter growth forecast to 6.6%q/q saar (previously: 5.5%q/q saar). In yoy terms, current-quarter real GDP growth should be 4.6% (previously: 4.3%oya). We maintain our growth forecast in the coming quarters (4.7%q/q saar in 2Q, 4.5% in 3Q and 4.1% in 4Q). Our full-year GDP growth forecast now stands at 5.2% (previously: 4.9%).

Despite a strong start in real activity, deflator pressure persists. We expect the GDP deflator to continue to be negative, at -1.5%oya in the current quarter. Hence, nominal GDP growth should soften further, to 3.1%oya (vs. 3.7%oya in 4Q23 and 3.5%oya in 3Q23). In other words, the passthrough from improved real activity to improved corporate earnings will still take time.

## Hong Kong unemployment rate at 2.9% sa

Hong Kong<sup>’</sup>s unemployment rate stayed at 2.9% sa for the three months ending February (Figure 2). Total employment fell 2.3k, to 3,694.7k, while the labor force fell 2.7k, to 3,797.0k. Seasonally adjusted, total employment rose 0.3%m m sa, while the labor force rose 0.1%m/m sa. The underemployment rate remained at 1.0%. It is worth noting that the seasonally adjusted labor force and employment growth finally turned positive after four consecutive months of decline. However, the sustainability of the recovery remains to be seen, as the LNY seasonal factor may have disrupted the data.

Figure 2: Hong Kong unemployment rate
![](images/2fb4b5e238602872e51a4ccbf6be12ff2ab7a55fcf2644cab4c2928b176fc095.jpg)

The SME business receipt current diffusion index (DI) rose 0.6pt, to 43.9 in February, while the outlook DI rose 1.3pt, to 46.6. SME employment DI edged down 0.1pt, to 48.8, suggesting marginal deterioration of employment sentiment in SME businesses. Meanwhile, total economy PMI fell 0.2pt, to 49.7, while the employment component of PMI rose 0.7pt, to 50.7. Overall, the city<sup>’</sup>s unemployment rate has returned to the lowest level since the onset of the pandemic, and we expect it to remain around 3% in the near term.

## CPI inflation at 2.1%oya

Hong Kong SAR<sup>’</sup>s headline CPI inflation rose to 2.1%oya in February (Figure 3), compared to 1.7%oya in January. Seasonally adjusted, we estimate that headline CPI rose 0.1%m/ m sa in February (vs. -0.2%m/m sa in January). CPI inflation tends to show greater volatility in the first two months of a year due to the timing of the Lunar New Year (LNY started on 10 February in 2024, but on 22 January in 2023). For January-February combined, CPI inflation rose 1.9%oya on an average loss of 0.2%m/m sa.

Figure 3: Hong Kong headline CPI
![](images/2ef2749b9adaa491be64776e0e77bae8ea384c0682ee5560f92df18663620165.jpg)

More than 45% of Hong Kong<sup>’</sup>s merchandise imports are from Mainland China. Mainland China<sup>’</sup>s February CPI spiked to 0.7%oya on the LNY effect, with food prices rebounding +1.6% m/m sa. Domestically, housing prices contribution to the year-on-year CPI inflation rose significantly in the past five months (due mainly to rising rental prices). On cross-border mobilities, the number of inbound visitors from the Mainland averaged 84.0% of the pre-pandemic level (2015-18 average) in February (vs. 70.2% in January), while the number of outbound HK residents averaged 114.6% of the pre-pandemic level. The imbalance in cross-border travel activities continued into the first 20 days of March. Looking ahead, we maintain our view that overall inflation in HK should stay moderate in the near term.

## Taiwan export orders fell 6.9%m/m sa

Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s export orders undershot market expectations, falling 10.4%oya or 6.9%m/m sa in February, following the 24.6% rebound in January. January-February export orders on average were 20.3% higher than the December level. Tech orders led the slide, falling 6.2%m/m sa, following the 28.9% surge in January, with broad-based easing in orders for electronics products, as well as information and communication products. Non-tech orders inched down 0.5% m/m sa, following the 6.8% solid expansion in January. By source of demand, the easing in February export orders was broad-based across the U.S. (-6.1% m/m sa) and the ASEAN region (-31.1% m/m sa), China/HK SAR (-5.3% m/m sa) and Europe (-0.7% m/m sa). Japan saw a modest 1.8% gain.

Figure 4: Taiwan export orders (level)
![](images/73c293b93f2e73eb4013b27250b158a2b39d5c3688f0788a5fda68868113eef7.jpg)

Our forecasts look for moderate growth in tech exports, including a structural boost of AI-related products and HPCrelated demand, as well as improving consumer demand in the coming quarters, while the tech sector<sup>’</sup>s de-stocking pressure should start to ease. With regard to the seemingly diverging signals from export sector indicators in recent months, we think they partly reflect the latest boost of AI-related tech demand, which relates to products that are mostly produced in Taiwan manufacturing plants, while overall export orders may be somewhat restrained by ongoing regional supply chain adjustments. Despite the modest retreat in February, partly on LNY-related seasonality, January and February as a whole still point to solid expansion compared to December. This is encouraging, suggesting near-term solid performance in tech exports for Taiwan and possibly Mainland China, as well.

## A surprising 12.5bp hike

Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s central bank raised the key policy rate by 12.5bps at Thursday<sup>’</sup>s quarterly monetary policy meeting (Figure 5). With this surprising move, the discount rate, the key policy rate in Taiwan, rose to 2.0%, the highest level since late 2008. We see the CBC<sup>’</sup>s hike as a pre-emptive move, aiming to curb inflation expectations, as inflation may stay sticky with the cost pass-through after electricity tariff hikes take place. The central bank has lifted its 2024 average CPI inflation forecast to 2.16%yoy.

On the economy<sup>’</sup>s inflation outlook, we expect CPI inflation to average around 2.4%oya in the coming three to six months and moderate to below 2% toward year-end; we expect headline CPI inflation to average 2024 at 2.1% and core CPI infla tion to average 2.0%. On the growth outlook, following the significant 9.7% q/q saar expansion in 4Q23, we expect the Taiwan economy to show moderate growth, with the 1H average pace at 2.2% q/q saar and 2H at 2.6%. Our 2024 real GDP growth forecast stands at 4.3%y/y.

With solid growth and expected inflation moderation, our forecast looks for the central bank to keep policy rates on hold for the rest of 2024. The CBC<sup>’</sup>s estimates suggest that full-year CPI inflation would likely rise 0.4%pt if the electricity tariff hike goes to 15%. This would further delay the timing for CPI inflation to fall to the “below 2%<sup>”</sup> comfortable range of the CBC. Hence, it will be key to watch CPI inflation prints in the coming months. If a major upside surprise is delivered for CPI, we think another hike in June can<sup>’</sup>t be ruled out.

Figure 5: Taiwan central bank policy rate and US Fed funds rate % per annum

## Korea

• Customs exports growth expected to slow in 1Q24

• Core PPI inflation continues to stabilize

• Next week: February IP expected to be flat m/m

Major trading partners<sup>’</sup> growth momentum in early 2024 looks better than we had assumed, but Korea<sup>’</sup>s customs exports growth is likely to moderate in 1Q24. Easing export growth is a breather after 4Q23<sup>’</sup>s strong growth, in our view, and a gradual recovery trend should continue in mid-year with sustained tech sector gains. As exports trend growth is moderating, we expect 1Q<sup>’</sup>s manufacturing production growth to also moderate. Next week<sup>’</sup>s February IP is expected to be flat after January<sup>’</sup>s 1.3%m/m, sa fall, and the threemonth trend growth should moderate. While semiconductor and tech production is expected to rebound after a brief fall in January, non-tech (such as autos) production is expected to stay weak to offset the tech production gain.

## Exports growth to moderate

Customs exports rose 11.2%oya in the first 20 days of March, and we estimate that full-month exports will rise 4.2%oya if trading day differences are controlled for. Seasonally adjusted, 20-day exports fell 1.3%m/m, sa in March to suggest a continued correction in exports growth by end-1Q24. If customs exports have fallen in March, as hinted by the flash data, then 1Q exports growth will mark 0.9%3m/3m, saar, after strong 24.5% growth in 4Q23 (Figure 1). Both tech and nontech exports trend growth moderated by February after 4Q23<sup>’</sup>s strong record, suggesting a broad-based slowdown in exports growth.

Figure 1: Korea customs exports
![](images/9ee1237d4020a658e36ce1e7e210053bc64584401732a0e38a089e25426b9e4b.jpg)

That said, customs imports should contract by 11.5%3m/3m, saar in March, in our estimation, according to the flash data by mid-March, and we continue to expect a positive contribution from net exports to overall GDP growth. The seasonally adjusted customs trade balance was estimated to mark US\$3.4bn sa in March, and the customs trade balance should continue on a gradual recovery trend amid monthly volatility.

## Core PPI continues disinflation trend

Producer prices rose 0.2%m/m, sa, in February, following increases of 0.4% in December and 0.2% in January, gradually slowing from a peak of 0.8% in August. Core PPI exhibited continuing stability, as it rose 0.2% in February after rising by an average of 0.2% in the prior four months. Services prices maintained a stable 0.2% pace for the seventh month in a row, while prices of non-oil industrial goods also rose 0.2% in February after rising 0.0-0.4% in the prior four months (Figure 2). Sequentially, core PPI inflation stood at 2.2% 3m/3m, saar, in February, after stabilizing from 3.6% in November to the current level in January. This is broadly in line with our view that core prices should lead a moderation in overall inflation.

Figure 2: Korea PPI - core items
![](images/3a8c402a9f089c5b7a016c60adaec86553d3361d0cb22f129b77fd5c1a84e0a1.jpg)

That said, other details hinted at a risk that further disinflation might be delayed by supply-side pressures in the near term, after their easing previously helped lowering inflation. Energy PPI rose 0.6%m/m following rises of 1.1% in December and 0.1% in January (Figure 3), reflecting a recovery in global crude oil prices from corrections in 4Q23.

Figure 3: Korea PPI - non-core items
![](images/735e199e2796743809692ef3793663e776aa20895109686ad11b2eaadaaca9f3.jpg)

While our commodities team continues to expect a very gradual rise in oil prices this year, recent price moves were a bit faster than anticipated. Meanwhile, farm products PPI rose 1.2% in February, with prices of fresh foods rising strongly 4.5% in February for the fifth monthly gain, despite being within the category<sup>’</sup>s usual high volatility.

## ASEAN

• Malaysia<sup>’</sup>s recent data convey ongoing resilience in domestic demand while external demand slows

• Financial account in focus despite current account surplus amid DM high-for-long rates

• Recent measures could help temper FX volatility, but longer-term measures likely needed

• Fiscal reform would likely be the first of several

Malaysian data suggest that domestic demand has been resilient despite the lackluster external demand environment. Although exports have been broadly stable, imports have firmed, leading to an annualized narrowing in the customs trade surplus to US\$29 billion in February from an average run rate of US\$50 billion last year. The rise in imports comes mainly from intermediate and capital goods and speaks to a build-out of domestic fixed asset investment. Whether this results in an eventual rise in production capacity for external or domestic demand remains to be seen. The narrowing in the trade surplus has also been reflected in the 4Q23 current account, which narrowed to a US\$53 million surplus from a prior two-year average quarterly run rate of US\$2.6 billon.

In a potentially high-for-long rates environment in the DM, this places greater focus on the financial accounts, which in Malaysia<sup>’</sup>s case has generally been in deficit. These pressures have been evident in the exchange rate, with the NEER falling by 3.4% since end-2022. Although Bank Negara has been able to manage these pressures, the policy degrees of freedom to continue doing so remain unclear, especially if further reforms to bolster confidence are not forthcoming.

## Narrowing trade surplus raises challenges

The recent trade data depict an economy where external demand has been lackluster while domestic demand has been resilient, especially capital imports (Figures 1 and 2). While this speaks to a potential firming in domestic investment, the implication is that this would also narrow the current account surplus, evident in the recent narrowing in the 4Q23 BOP data (Figure 3). In Malaysia<sup>’</sup>s case, the balance of payments has been characterized by a relatively stable current account surplus and a financial account that has been more volatile. In particular, the portfolio and other investment flows tend to dominate the financial account amid persistent outflows in the errors and omissions line item, which speaks to the behavioral bias of residents (Figure 4). These developments are likely to be exacerbated by an external environment that could remain a headwind, especially if DM monetary conditions remain high for long.

Figure 1: Malaysia trade US\$ billion, 6mma, saar, both scales
![](images/c70831e0487b6b6c723042eacdadad66a64bb82a3f54aa79b614f0521f477be3.jpg)

Figure 2: Malaysia imports by end-use Index, 2019=100, 3mma, sa
![](images/e8c6b8dc656f7ec46fc089efbf046340faa44f4bbc93c96c1fd63f0418259afa.jpg)

Figure 3: Malaysia balance of payments US\$ billion, 4-quarter sum
![](images/7c005320146c68662bedb80640513feb53bf41049f2d790827cdc380347b5c35.jpg)

Figure 4: Malaysia financial account US\$ billion, 4-quarter sum
![](images/a16d714a51e3697a9e1c334c63457a585cc9667f0a1bbc966ee78219550588c9.jpg)

## BNM FX operations in focus

Given these pressures in the past couple of years, Malaysia<sup>’</sup>s NEER has experienced some weakening, accompanied by a material decline in net FX reserves (Figures 5 and 6). However, gross FX reserves have been more stable and reflect the operations of Bank Negara through its monetary operations, which have included operations that absorb excess onshore US\$ liquidity via deposits or through its FX forward/swap operations. These FX forward/swap operations had in the past also mitigated the impact of foreign bond outflows on financial stability, which is a policy mandate of Bank Negara (Figure 7).

Figure 5: Malaysia spot FX and NEER US\$/MYR
![](images/51be172e92dabaddca4342bd93b74e5e081da9b30a75cc078a8f8efe53799605.jpg)

Figure 6: Malaysia FX reserves
![](images/7522c70875e3a815dddd477ac7259caa42ba48e219071c296d6526101b87c9da.jpg)

Figure 7: Malaysia pre-determined drains on FX reserves US\$ billion
![](images/0e003b427b27f27394ff8973fdc0a852518f0a0e4f40e898efe8ece6f5a61405.jpg)

BNM<sup>’</sup>s US\$ “buy-sell<sup>’</sup> operations provided a conduit for injecting MYR liquidity to banks in return for US\$ and had the dual impact of providing BNM access to onshore US\$ and, at the same time, providing MYR funding for banks to buy bonds, thus offsetting the impact of foreign bond outflows. This has been evident in the concurrent rise in bank bond holdings in 2017 and late 2018 amid a decline in foreign bond holdings and a rise in BNM<sup>’</sup>s short FX forward US\$ position (Figures 8 and 9). More recently, BNM<sup>’</sup>s FX forward operations in 2022/23 have led to a rise in its government bond holdings, providing indirect support for the bond market.

Figure 8: Malaysia local currency govt. bond ownership
![](images/e0b1d19f2201507029dce9bb57687ea712c8298229f9deaad52d3663a6c1bc06.jpg)

Figure 9: Malaysia bank bond holding and FX forwards
![](images/c1034b4f85b90445d5050df25f266d9e98b2d5cb658fcca8982a30c693a2c5e3.jpg)

Figure 10: Malaysia banking system foreign currency position US\$ billion,both scales
![](images/19446de5e117548214767c57d5d9412652a19652a32e3bceb2716dafabad8905.jpg)

However, there are limits to these operations, approximated by the volume of excess foreign currency – mainly US\$ – liquidity in the onshore banking system. Having risen materially since 2020, this looks to be stabilizing at around US\$30 billion (Figure 10). These constraints could have been a factor behind the measures announced on 1 March, which encouraged Government Linked Entities to repatriate and convert their offshore investment income into local currency. However, this too faces constraints, given that Malaysia<sup>’</sup>s net international position – excluding FX reserves – is negative (Table 1).

## BNM under strain; reforms needed

These developments bring us to the fundamental question of why domestic investors have shied away from investing domestically despite ostensibly resilient growth. In our view, this likely reflects more broadly on structural issues that need to be addressed. Until these issues are resolved, Bank Negara will be required to manage a potentially conflicting set of objectives: the balance of payments, fx and inflation, while still maintaining financial stability. To ease these strains, fiscal reforms, starting with subsidy rationalization, would be a useful first step, but would need to be accompanied by other reforms to deal with the investment climate.

## India

• Merchandise trade deficit widens in February on gold imports surge, but export performance remains strong

• Both merchandise and service exports see strong growth

• Net service exports post 19% growth for the first 11 months of this fiscal year

The February trade report was constructive. The merchandise trade deficit widened due to a surge in gold imports, but not to threatening levels. Furthermore, non-oil exports continued to improve smartly, even as non-oil non-gold (NoNG) imports eased in February. All this meant that the underlying trade deficit (ex oil and gold) was the lowest in 30 months. Service exports growth was also strong, taking net services exports to record levels in February. Net service exports have seen a significant re-acceleration in the last three months.

## Trade deficit widens in February

After narrowing for three months, the merchandise trade deficit widened from \$16.5bn to \$18.7bn (Figure 1), driven largely by a surge in gold imports from \$2.4bn in January to \$7.9bn in February. Additionally, net oil imports rose to \$8.7bn in February after averaging \$7.6bn in the prior three months.

Figure 1: Trade deficit
![](images/c1de2937f85bc0777398f935c5ff7518629193d7dded06403556632344f83725.jpg)

Looking past the pickup in gold and oil imports, however, non-oil exports continued to firm, a trend seen in recent months. Sequentially, non-oil exports grew at solid 9.7% m/ m, sa on the back of 1.7% contraction last month, taking the last four months<sup>’</sup> sequential growth average to an impressive 3.7% m/m, sa. Meanwhile, NoNG imports contracted sequentially by 4.1% in February, giving back some of the firming of the last few months. Strong exports and weaker NoNG imports meant that the trade deficit ex oil and gold reached the lowest in 30 months, at just \$2.2bn.

Figure 2: Exports ex. oil
![](images/e6873b20b51c95d767b14b7f6ab48f340cd50c833d419a719e226eb2b355b4a2.jpg)
Source: Ministry of Trade & Commerce J.P. Morgan

## Services exports accelerate

Services exports rose to \$32.1bn in February, inching up from \$31bn in January (Figure 3). In oya terms, however, services were an impressive 17% higher than the previous year. More generally, service exports have seen a sharp re-acceleration over the last three months, reflected in the 32% 3m/3m, saar. Similarly, service imports growth has also accelerated over the last three months. But even accounting for that, net service exports rose sharply, to a record \$16.8bn in February.

Figure 3: Gross Service Exports
![](images/3c1382c9bb228a9a42fcaf5cb4fb30e67a725fce40137e35393b7afee6b3679a.jpg)

Figure 4: Combined (merchandise and services) trade deficit \$ bn, 3mma
![](images/16d9861c142cba2a3112dea2914bd8ce2318c0b5af1328ed168edd2cf80972d2.jpg)

Net service exports were 19% higher in the first 11 months of the fiscal year than in the previous year. The robust performance of services exports, combined with a contained merchandise trade deficit, resulted in a modest combined trade and services deficit of only \$2bn. While this represents an increase from \$300mn in January, it remains historically low

(Figure 4).
